Title: SideTaskBench: Measuring Unauthorized Side-Task Delivery in Coding Agents

  • Authors: Xiangyu Wen, Qiang Xu
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14548
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.14548
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.14548]] SideTaskBench: Measuring Unauthorized Side-Task Delivery in Coding Agents(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14548)
  • Keywords: defense
  • Abstract: Can a coding agent fix a user's repository while also completing and delivering work the user never requested? In this paper, we introduce SideTaskBench to measure this failure where third-party technical references induce agents to produce independent side-task artifacts during an authorized repair. Repair success alone misses this behavior. Our key measurement contribution is to pair each repair with a self-contained side task and verify side tasks' delivery success rate along with the repair success rate. The benchmark contains 25 Python repairs and 190 side-task units. Its four-level similarity index defines 300 executable repair--side-task pairs. The experiments show roughly 40% valid delivery under wrapped requests for two models, alongside repair success close to their clean baselines. The completed v10 similarity run uses DeepSeek-V4-Flash on all 300 pairs, with eight repetitions per pair (2,400 runs). Conditional delivery ranges from 20.48% for L0 unrelated tasks to 57.44% for L3 mechanism-overlapping tasks, while the L1-L0 difference is 5.37 percentage points and remains sensitive to task composition. The central insight is that technical relevance may blur the authorization boundary for an agent, such that an agent can solve the requested problem and still deliver unauthorized work. SideTaskBench emphasizes this potential weakness of existing agents and provides a testbed for defenses that preserve repair capability while preventing unauthorized delivery. The code is available at: this https URL.

Title: An Asynchronous Federated Learning Platform for Lung Adenocarcinoma Grading via Foundation Models and Structural Pruning

  • Authors: Moon-Il Joo, Md Ariful Islam Mozumder, Hee-Cheol Kim
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14590
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.14590
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.14590]] An Asynchronous Federated Learning Platform for Lung Adenocarcinoma Grading via Foundation Models and Structural Pruning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14590)
  • Keywords: privacy, protect, federate
  • Abstract: Classification of lung adenocarcinoma into poorly differentiated (PD) and well-to-moderately differentiated (WMD) subtypes plays an important role in patient survival; however, consistent classification is difficult due to inter-observer variability among pathologists. Collecting data from multiple hospitals is also challenging due to patient privacy concerns. This study proposes a web-based federated learning system that shares only locally trained model weights without sharing patient data between institutions. The UNI2 foundation model and CLAM-SB were used to classify pathological whole slide images. A four-client federated learning system was constructed, and the NLST cohort was reserved exclusively for external validation. In centralized evaluation, joint training with CPTAC and TCGA outperformed single-institution training on external validation, confirming the necessity of multi-institutional data integration. FedAvg, FedProx, FedBN, and FedNova were compared as federated learning algorithms under identical experimental conditions. Test AUC ranked as FedBN, FedProx, FedNova, and FedAvg. FedNova showed the most balanced classification performance between PD and WMD. L1-norm structural pruning at 20%, 30%, and 40% sparsity was applied to reduce model transmission size. FedAvg, FedProx, and FedBN showed a tendency of decreasing Test AUC under pruning, while FedNova achieved improved Test AUC across all sparsity levels. A web-based application was developed supporting slide upload, local training, AI classification, and attention heatmap-based visualization. The combination of FedNova and 40% pruning was confirmed as a practical approach that improves communication efficiency and classification performance while protecting patient privacy.

Title: Adversarial Risk in the Generative AI Era Necessitates Dropping the Small Epsilon Ball

  • Authors: Andrew C. Cullen, Neil G. Marchant, Paul Montague, Jiani Xie, Benjamin I.P. Rubinstein
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14594
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.14594
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.14594]] Adversarial Risk in the Generative AI Era Necessitates Dropping the Small Epsilon Ball(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14594)
  • Keywords: security, attack, robust, generative
  • Abstract: Adversarial Machine Learning research is currently centered on the search for the epsilon-ball: minimal perturbations designed to fool models while remaining imperceptible to humans. We argue that this fixation on $\ell_p$ norms is a metrological trap that communicates a distorted perception of risk to defenders, one that is unaligned with real-world system vulnerabilities. This paradigm misleads defenders into optimizing for narrow, mathematical robustness at the expense of systemic security, while simultaneously incentivizing attackers to exploit non-overlapping alternative adversarial pathways. Ultimately, we argue that the dominance of the $\ell_p$ ball does not just fail to reduce harm---it may actively facilitate it by communicating a false sense of security against the wrong threats.

Title: KernelMosaic: An Open Dataset and Agentic Framework for CUDA Kernel Generation

  • Authors: Ningyuan Xi, Yuhang Lai, Ning Miao
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14604
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.14604
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.14604]] KernelMosaic: An Open Dataset and Agentic Framework for CUDA Kernel Generation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14604)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Training large language models to generate efficient CUDA kernels requires supervision spanning diverse computational requirements and iterative code optimization. Constructing this supervision is challenging because of heterogeneous specifications, and useful implementations emerge through iterative complicated testing and refinement. We introduce KernelMosaic, an open dataset pairing diverse CUDA tasks with verified solutions and trajectories. Human-guided review and diversity-aware selection curate tasks expressed as natural-language descriptions, reference programs, mathematical specifications, and their intersections. To build the solutions and trajectories, our agentic framework guides a strong LLM through implementation, correctness repair, and performance optimization with reusable CUDA guidance and execution feedback. Supervised fine-tuning of a small open-source model on KernelMosaic raises correctness from 4%, 0%, and 0% to 88%, 82%, and 16% on KernelBench L1, L2, and L3, respectively, while increasing the fraction of tasks with kernels faster than PyTorch eager at every level. Further training experiments show that supervision from the curated trajectories substantially outperforms direct responses and promotes sustained testing and revision. These results demonstrate that KernelMosaic enables a general-purpose model to achieve substantial gains in CUDA kernel generation.

Title: Thompson Sampling using Prior-fitted Diffusion Transformers

  • Authors: Sihwa Park, Jingsen Zhu, Vinamr Jain, Sheng-Yen Chou, Alexander Terenin
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, stat.ML
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14656
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.14656
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.14656]] Thompson Sampling using Prior-fitted Diffusion Transformers(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14656)
  • Keywords: diffusion, transformer
  • Abstract: Thompson sampling is natural randomized strategy for optimizing unknown functions in a black-box manner. In continuous global optimization, Thompson sampling has largely been restricted to Gaussian process priors, as these are essentially the only class for which computing the posterior distribution has been tractable in practice. In this work, we introduce \emph{prior-fitted diffusion transformers}, which are diffusion models that perform Thompson sampling in-context at inference time, after being pre-trained using the prior-fitted network paradigm. To apply these models, we develop a set of practical non-Gaussian priors for general black-box functions---including those that have characteristics that cannot be achieved under Gaussianity, such as priors over unimodal functions. We also quantify the tradeoff between model size and the number of pre-training iterations, and show that these models follow scaling laws akin to those observed in transformers defined over other modalities. Across a set of global optimization benchmarks, we show that prior-fitted diffusion transformers with non-Gaussian priors can achieve improved performance compared to traditional pipelines built on Gaussian processes.

Title: Closing the Loop: Co-Evolving EM for Irregular Time Series Generation in Lifted Representations

  • Authors: Idan Arbiv, Gal Fadlon, Omri Azencot
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14659
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.14659
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.14659]] Closing the Loop: Co-Evolving EM for Irregular Time Series Generation in Lifted Representations(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14659)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Regular time-series generation from irregular time series is typically reduced to one-shot imputation: complete each partial sequence once, freeze the completions, and train a generator on the resulting surrogate dataset. We show that this open-loop strategy is structurally flawed. Point imputers collapse ambiguous missing regions to conditional averages, while stochastic imputers become stale as the generator evolves; the binding constraint is therefore iteration, not imputer design. We introduce a co-evolving Monte Carlo EM framework that closes the imputer-generator loop. Each E-step samples missing values from the posterior of the current generator, and each M-step retrains the generator on these completions, preserving unconditional generator training. Since our diffusion prior operates in a lifted 2D representation while observations live in time-series space, we further introduce Posterior Sampling for Lifted Representations (PSLR), which enforces operator-consistent conditioning, uncertainty preservation, and manifold consistency during the E-step. Across ten datasets and four corruption regimes, our method achieves state-of-the-art performance, improving over the strongest baselines by 59% in discriminative score, 15% in predictive score, 73% in Context-FID, and 71% in feature-correlation error. Ablations show that our approach reduces average discriminative score by 73% over single-step baselines, closing most of the remaining gap to the clean-data oracle. Code is available at this https URL.

Title: Beyond Row-Level Prediction: Evaluating and Learning Composition for Reusable Table Representations

  • Authors: Ali Younes, Saeed Ghoorchian, Maximilian Schambach, Georgia Chalvatzaki, Johannes Höhne
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14697
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.14697
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.14697]] Beyond Row-Level Prediction: Evaluating and Learning Composition for Reusable Table Representations(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14697)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Tables are compositional, with content spread across a schema, cell values, rows, and columns. Large-scale operations such as retrieval, overlap estimation, deduplication, and data curation need these local signals composed into one reusable embedding vector per table. Table representation methods, however, are mostly evaluated through row-level prediction or supervised downstream tasks, which do not measure table-level embedding quality directly. We introduce a benchmark that evaluates table-level embeddings through four desiderata: consistency under partial views, discriminability across label granularities, robustness to benign perturbations, and efficiency. Across twelve methods on synthetic and real corpora, lightweight schema- and text-based fingerprints are among the strongest. The sharpest contrast comes from two tabular foundation models, TabPFN and ConTextTab: although both are strong row-level predictors, their states, composed by mean, max, or three further parameter-free pooling rules, trail the strongest fingerprints. We therefore ask whether the gap lies in the encoder or in the compositional rule. Keeping ConTextTab frozen, we learn only the compositional rule over its cell states, using support-conditioned distribution matching (SCDM), a self-supervised loss we propose that matches the distribution of embedding similarities to the distribution of partition overlaps within and across tables. Across all fifteen head configurations, the learned rule raises the Spearman correlation between embedding similarity and partial-view overlap from 0.04 to 0.43-0.50 on synthetic data and improves triplet ranking, clustering, and retrieval, with smaller but systematic gains on real data. A downstream parent-table retrieval task confirms these findings. How local states are composed, and with what objective, substantially affects the quality of table-level embeddings.

Title: Incrementally Verifiable Oracle Aided Computation

  • Authors: Shafi Goldwasser, Yael Kalai
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14698
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.14698
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.14698]] Incrementally Verifiable Oracle Aided Computation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14698)
  • Keywords: security, robust
  • Abstract: Incrementally verifiable computation (IVC) allows a prover to maintain a short proof that can be efficiently updated after each computation step, without retaining or recomputing the preceding execution. Standard IVC schemes do not directly address a crucial feature of AI tasks: their dependence on external information and expertise. We construct IVC schemes for oracle-aided computations under the LWE assumption by first constructing SNARGs, and then extending them to IVC. We consider the following two settings, where below, k is the security parameter, n is the input length, and T, S, and Q are the time, space, and oracle-query bounds. 1. Robust computations: For bounded-space computations, including non-deterministic computations, in which every accepting purported execution on a false input contains at least an eps-fraction of incorrect oracle answers, we construct privately verifiable SNARGs and IVC. Public parameters, private verification keys, and proofs have length poly(k ,S, log T,1/eps), and verification uses O(k/eps) oracle calls. We complement this result with a lower bound ruling out (deterministic) publicly verifiable SNARGs and IVCs with public parameters of length o(n), and o(Q) verifier oracle queries. 2. Signed oracles: For oracles that digitally sign their query-answer pairs, we construct publicly verifiable SNARGs and IVC without a robustness assumption, and with oracle-free setup and verification. Public parameters and proofs have length poly(k,S,log T). For deterministic computations with oracle query and answer lengths poly(k,log T), we remove the dependence on S, obtaining public parameter and proof lengths poly(k,log T). All our constructions have adaptive completeness and soundness.

Title: Code as Explanations: Executable and Verifiable LLM Explanations

  • Authors: Nhi Nguyen, Shauli Ravfogel, Rajesh Ranganath
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL, stat.ML
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14704
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.14704
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.14704]] Code as Explanations: Executable and Verifiable LLM Explanations(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14704)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: As large language models (LLMs) increasingly make consequential decisions and solve problems alongside humans, we must understand how models arrive at their conclusions. Understanding how an LLM behaves requires observing it across multiple inputs, yet free-text explanations such as chain-of-thought (CoT) are generated per instance and cannot be easily verified on new inputs. We propose {code explanations to address the mismatch in how LLM explanations are generated versus how they are evaluated: a program explains LLM behavior across a dataset of inputs, and its execution enables automatic verification. We define Consistency metric that measures the fraction of inputs on which the program reproduces the LLM's outputs, and use it to evaluate and select candidate programs. Across three open-source code-capable LLMs and eight tasks, we find that LLMs can produce highly consistent code explanations in several settings. Code explanations are also 6$\times$ to 100$\times$ shorter than CoT, and better align with model behavior in 9 out of 24 settings. However, consistency is significantly lower on tasks with more complex linguistic components, where generated programs reproduce model outputs no more frequently than an uninformative explanation baseline, and these programs often rely on longer hard-coded patterns. In experiments with Claude Opus 5.5, providing the model with consistency feedback and code execution improves explanation consistency across tasks, but consistency remains significantly lower on more complex tasks. Our findings establish executable code as a novel, verifiable, and compact approach to explaining LLM behavior and highlight verification feedback as a promising direction for improving explanation consistency. The code for this paper is at this https URL.

Title: SyntheIce: A Synthetic Radargram Annotation Benchmark for Englacial Layer Reconstruction

  • Authors: Muhammad Behroze Hassan, Bayu Adhi Tama, Sanjay Purushotham, Vandana P. Janeja
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14728
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.14728
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.14728]] SyntheIce: A Synthetic Radargram Annotation Benchmark for Englacial Layer Reconstruction(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14728)
  • Keywords: extraction
  • Abstract: Englacial layers in radio-echo-sounding radargrams (subsurface ice images) encode the climate and flow history of ice sheets and glaciers. However, large-scale extraction is hindered by time-consuming manual annotation and semi-automated detectors that produce fragmented rather than continuous horizons. We address two challenges: reconstructing complete layers from partial detections across radargram profiles and validating layer continuity across adjacent tile boundaries. We introduce a slope-aware greedy fragment-linking algorithm that reconstructs full-width layers using row-similarity gating and an exponentially weighted moving average (EWMA) slope predictor. Each reconstruction receives a content-based hash ID for stable cross-radargram identification. For quantitative evaluation, we generate SyntheIce, a synthetic dataset of 600 radargrams spanning multiple complexity levels and realistic noise conditions, including 150 wide radargrams divided into four adjacent tiles. All samples include exact ground-truth annotations. We evaluate the dataset using XCheck, a consistency-based validation framework, extending its original two-image setting to four consecutive tiles and enforcing continuity at every boundary. This demonstrates the dataset's utility for developing and evaluating real-world annotation algorithms. Our findings extend beyond ice science: when spatial features are fragmented but retain consistent local geometry, trajectory-based linking is more reliable than appearance-based matching. Evaluation against expert-labeled real radargrams demonstrates high precision, recall, F1-score, and mean IoU, supporting the method's transfer to real-world data.

Title: Benchmarking Multilingual Document Understanding in Vision-Language Models

  • Authors: Matteo Gabburo, Daniela Baiamonte, Stefano Simonazzi, Silvia Gardini, Sean Cesare Lazzeri, Andrea Zugarini
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14758

  • Copy Paste: [[2611.14758]] Benchmarking Multilingual Document Understanding in Vision-Language Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14758)
  • Keywords: extraction
  • Abstract: Recent Vision-Language Models (VLMs) perform strongly on multimodal tasks, Document Understanding (DU) among them, but the benchmarks that measure them mostly cover English documents and narrow problems. How well current VLMs generalize across languages, tasks, styles and layouts therefore remains unclear. We introduce MDU, a multilingual DU benchmark of 23146 examples spanning 13 tasks and five languages: English, French, German, Italian, and Spanish. The tasks isolate complementary capabilities, from surface-level text and Markdown extraction through summarization, classification and named-entity recognition to layout-sensitive passage and layout extraction. We evaluate 45 state-of-the-art open-weight VLMs from 1.7B to 235B parameters, plus one commercial system under the same protocol. The benchmark is far from saturated: the best average is 69.0, only eight of the 45 evaluated systems reach 65, and the two best results come from 27B models rather than from the largest systems we evaluate. Performance is uneven in ways that scale does not fix. The gap between the strongest and weakest language does not close as models grow, and it survives controlling for the domain composition of each language. Capabilities also do not improve together: once we partial out overall model quality, per-task scores are largely uncorrelated, and transcription ability in particular trades off against semantic extraction.

Title: Graph Label Alignment: A Diagnostic Atlas for Graph Classification

  • Authors: Neelam Akula, Varun Shiralkar, Murat Kantarcioglu, Baris Coskunuzer
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14760
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.14760
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.14760]] Graph Label Alignment: A Diagnostic Atlas for Graph Classification(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14760)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Graph classification benchmarks vary widely in difficulty: some are solved by simple pooled baselines, while others remain challenging for GNNs and graph transformers. We take a data-centric view and ask whether benchmark success is driven by genuine structure--feature reasoning or by label signal already accessible from restricted marginal views. We introduce graph label alignment, a lightweight diagnostic that measures label accessibility through two supervised probes computed without training a graph encoder: structural label alignment (SLA), based on permutation-invariant topological and spectral summaries, and feature label alignment (FLA), based on pooled node features with edges removed. Across real graph classification benchmarks and multiple pooled backbones, the resulting SLA--FLA plane strongly organizes test accuracy, with low-alignment datasets forming a consistent regime where standard pooled pipelines struggle. To probe what lies beyond marginal accessibility, we introduce controlled interaction-dominated stress tests, including heterophilic ego-graphs and synthetic Twins tasks whose structural and feature marginals are matched across classes but whose role--feature couplings differ. These results show that high alignment explains much of easy benchmark performance, while low alignment reveals regimes where success requires preserving structure--feature correspondence beyond standard pooled representations.

Title: Distributionally Robust BEST-RQ For Speech Representation Learning

  • Authors: Muhammad Khan, Xiaodong Cui, Brian Kingsbury, George Saon
  • Subjects: cs.CL, eess.AS, math.OC
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14801
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.14801
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.14801]] Distributionally Robust BEST-RQ For Speech Representation Learning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14801)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: BEST-RQ, based on random projection and quantization, has been a well-established self-supervised learning approach to learn speech representations from large-scale unlabeled audio due to its high performance and efficiency. However, in BEST-RQ representations learned by random quantization can be sensitive to the choice of quantization targets. To address this issue, we propose a distributionally robust extension of BEST-RQ that seeks representations robust across multiple quantization configurations. Specifically, we define groups by codebooks and formulate the training as a group Distributionally Robust Optimization (DRO) problem. Rather than optimizing solely for the average loss across groups, our approach adaptively reweights groups to emphasize those performing poorly. Additionally, we regularize DRO with a Kullback-Leibler divergence to prevent excessive distribution deviation. We evaluate our approach on the Multilingual LibriSpeech dataset and show that the distributionally robust BEST-RQ achieves superior performance over the conventional uniform average across multiple codebooks.

Title: OverServe: Resource Amplification Attacks on SaaS Agents via Tool--Prompt Co-Design

  • Authors: Ruiheng Yu, Oteo Mamo, Siyu Gan, Weikuan Yu, Mulong Luo
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.SE
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14808
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.14808
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.14808]] OverServe: Resource Amplification Attacks on SaaS Agents via Tool--Prompt Co-Design(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14808)
  • Keywords: attack, large language model
  • Abstract: Advances in large language models (LLMs) are enabling software-as-a-service (SaaS) providers to automate longer business workflows with agents. In subscription-based SaaS deployments, model and tool calls consume provider resources without additional customer payments. Because the agent autonomously decides when to stop, a malicious tenant can send a prompt to the agent, exploiting its completion decisions to extend execution and waste provider resources. In this paper, we present OverServe, a multi-agent framework for constructing such attacks against SaaS agents. OverServe co-designs Model Context Protocol (MCP) status tools and completion-confirmation prompts for the attacker. Each tool call returns accurate status and terminates normally, yet the agent may repeatedly check an unfinished condition to satisfy the prompt. This consumes the SaaS provider's resources even after the original business task is completed. OverServe constructs service-specific tool--prompt pairs and uses separate tool and prompt reviews to guide candidate pair selection. Tested tool specifications trigger no alerts in the evaluated MCP scanners. We evaluate Qwen, Nemotron, DeepSeek, and Sonnet on selected tasks from nine benchmarks. All OverServe runs pass the original task checks. OverServe can reach a maximal amplification of API requests by $93.88\times$ while preserving the original task goal and collateral checks.

Title: BirdDreams: Joint Perspective and BEV Video Generation for a Plausible, Reactive Driving World Model

  • Authors: Junyoung Seo, Jaewoo Seo, Seungryong Kim, Laura Leal-Taixé, Xiuming Zhang, Seung Wook Kim
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14815
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.14815
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.14815]] BirdDreams: Joint Perspective and BEV Video Generation for a Plausible, Reactive Driving World Model(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14815)
  • Keywords: diffusion, transformer
  • Abstract: Driving world models aim to simulate how a scene unfolds under different ego actions. This requires plausible motion of surrounding vehicles as well as precise ego-trajectory control. Learning vehicle motion from perspective video alone is challenging because motion is entangled with appearance and vehicles can be occluded. Prior work therefore conditions video generation on layouts that specify vehicle motion, providing control over surrounding vehicles but requiring their future trajectories as inputs. We introduce BirdDreams, an autoregressive driving world model that jointly generates perspective video and a schematic bird's-eye-view (BEV) video, making scene layout part of the predicted future. The BEV, rendered from scene annotations for training, depicts roads and vehicles as simple shapes at a fixed metric scale, providing a top-down view of their positions and motion with reduced occlusion between vehicles. Perspective video, by contrast, retains scene details that the BEV omits, such as traffic signals and object appearance. Joint attention between two video diffusion transformers, one per stream, lets each stream inform the other during generation. The predicted BEV also provides a geometric interface for ego control: we translate and rotate its latest frame along an input ego trajectory and use the resulting frames as a soft condition that shows how the scene would appear to move if only the ego vehicle moved. At inference, BirdDreams generates both videos from a single camera image without requiring an HD map or vehicle-state inputs. On PhysicalAI-AV and nuScenes, BirdDreams follows input ego trajectories more faithfully than prior driving world models and generates more plausible ego motion without ego-action conditioning. Human evaluation shows that BirdDreams outperforms a perspective-only baseline in generating realistic surrounding-vehicle behavior.

Title: Discharge Summary Generation Is Not Just Summarization: A Reality Check for Evaluation

  • Authors: Radhika Dua, Frederick Wolff, Kyunghyun Cho, Eric Karl Oermann
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14823
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.14823
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.14823]] Discharge Summary Generation Is Not Just Summarization: A Reality Check for Evaluation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14823)
  • Keywords: fair
  • Abstract: Discharge summaries combine a retrospective account of hospitalization with discharge-time decisions. Physician-written references therefore contain both facts supported by prior clinical notes and reference-only content, potentially reflecting clinical synthesis, handoffs, or discharge planning. Reference-only evaluation can conflate failure to recover available information with failure to reproduce content absent from the model input. We introduce FairSumEval, an evidence-aware, fact-level framework that assesses whether each reference and generated fact is supported by the model's evidence and captured by the other summary. The resulting outcome map separates matches, omissions, and additions. Two complementary metrics, Soft IoU-S and Soft IoU-NS, measure reference-generation agreement for facts with and without identified evidence support. On 200 MIMIC-III admissions, 18.8% of physician reference facts have identified support in the prior evidence, compared with 65.4% and 63.5% of facts generated by a general-purpose and a medically specialized model. Overlap is substantially higher for supported content (0.255 and 0.236) than for content without identified support (0.030 and 0.026). The two scores are weakly correlated across admissions (rho = 0.22 and 0.17), and conventional metrics do not consistently capture both dimensions. FairSumEval distinguishes evidence-grounded summarization from agreement on content without identified support, revealing what models omit and add in each category.

Title: LLMs and the Homogenization of Teaching

  • Authors: Mei Tan, Hyunji Nam, Hari Subramonyam, Dorottya Demszky
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14828
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.14828
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.14828]] LLMs and the Homogenization of Teaching(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14828)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are known to lack diversity in open-ended text generation, a limitation that is especially consequential in education, where homogeneity can narrow the kinds of thinking and disciplinary work students are invited to do. We study diversity in two educational generation tasks: math items and essay feedback. We compare frontier and open-weight models with human-authored reference distributions using lexical, semantic, and domain-grounded measures. For math, we examine the mathematical activities students are asked to perform; for feedback, we examine the revision work requested and the rhetorical strategies used to communicate it. We evaluate direct prompting, two diversity prompting methods, and post-training. Across both tasks, directly prompted model outputs are markedly less diverse than human-authored content--78% higher on lexical (Self-BLEU) and 51% higher on semantic (embedding) similarity--and represent a narrower range of instructional forms. Diversity prompting and post-training increase variation largely through surface features without broadening the instructional repertoire. Models inherit and amplify the most normative routines of human instruction. Overcoming mode collapse in education will require richer instructional data and investment in teachers' creative capacity and professional judgment.

Title: SPIRE: Task-agnostic embodied continual learning through autonomous situation inference and causal motor-primitive reuse

  • Authors: Yiyang Jia, Mingyang Lyu, Gongce Cao, Feifei Zhao, Yinqian Sun, Yi Zeng

  • Keywords: protect
  • Abstract: Embodied continual learning requires controllers to infer changing situations, retain prior behavior and reuse motor skills without task labels or known boundaries. The challenge is deciding when to recover earlier behavior and which skills can support new learning. We introduce Situation-guided Primitive Integration for Recurrent Embodiment (SPIRE), a recurrent-spiking system that infers latent contexts from action-outcome evidence and learns context-specific motor actors online. Mature actors become protected sources that combine with a target-context residual to drive recurrent-spiking motor populations. Across coordinate, three-joint and hidden-force control, SPIRE maintained control, autonomously inferred situations, retained prior behavior and accelerated acquisition of an unseen source composition, reducing mean interactions to criterion by approximately 40% relative to matched fresh controllers. Rewinding compatible source actors impaired early acquisition in every matched intervention, demonstrating causal reuse. Against seven recurrent continual-learning and modular-control comparators, SPIRE achieved 2.4 times the mean prior-learning advantage of the strongest eligible task-free alternative selected for each paired stream. These results demonstrate autonomous recovery and causal reuse of motor skills for new learning in continuous embodied control.

Title: Honest Fusion Evaluation for Cross-Domain Deepfake Detection: Leakage Audits and Tail Robustness

  • Authors: Luke Collins, Chang-Tsun Li, Duc Thanh Nguyen
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14856
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.14856
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.14856]] Honest Fusion Evaluation for Cross-Domain Deepfake Detection: Leakage Audits and Tail Robustness(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14856)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Cross-domain video deepfake detection increasingly uses frozen foundation-model experts, combined by calibrated score averaging, a per-video router picking one expert, or a learned head over concatenated features. Expressive routing and feature fusion are often reported to beat plain averaging. Across a 22-dataset frozen-expert testbed, we find those gains are largely evaluation artifacts. A corner theorem locates the gap routing aims to close: because AUC depends only on score ordering, the best any score-space router can do is an oracle reading the test label to pick each video's expert, a ceiling no realisable, label-blind router reaches. The theorem does not order real routers against a calibrated global average; the case against routing is empirical, and no router we test beats that average. Feature fusion reads raw features, falls outside the theorem, and is audited empirically. Reported gains trace to leakage: holding out one dataset leaks siblings sharing identities and reals (over-crediting routing), while holding out one manipulation keeps that dataset's reals in training (over-crediting feature fusion). A $2\times 2$ over fold and evaluation granularity pins the $+2.6$pp feature-fusion "win" to the fold alone: 12/12 significant under leaky folds vs. 0/12 under honest family-held-out folds, where both gains vanish. Fusion still pays off in the tail of hard videos and families: as experts are added, videos every expert misclassifies drop from 15.2% to 2.3%, and global calibrated fusion (InvB) beats the best single expert on worst-family AUC, selective risk (AURC), and low-false-positive recall, stably across protocols. Practitioners should calibrate and combine globally, hold out whole dataset families, and measure tail performance. For score-space routing under ranking metrics, honest evaluation and expert diversity matter more than combiner complexity.

  • Abstract: An AI agent's decision not to act can itself become an attack surface. Title: FOE-Dreamer: Deployment-Efficient Learning of Cyber Defense Policies in Operational Networks

  • Authors: Yinuo Du, Christopher Kiekintveld
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14880
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.14880
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.14880]] FOE-Dreamer: Deployment-Efficient Learning of Cyber Defense Policies in Operational Networks(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14880)
  • Keywords: defense, attack
  • Abstract: Autonomous cyber defense is widely studied with deep reinforcement learning, but most work trains policies inside simulators because learning requires many trial-and-error interactions. Simulator-based training carries a fidelity gap: trained policies face authentication delays, partial action failures, service dependencies, and user traffic patterns that the simulator did not represent, and degrade on transfer. Training directly in the network being defended removes the gap but makes every interaction slow and costly, so sample efficiency becomes the binding constraint. Model-based reinforcement learning has reduced sample requirements on games and physical robots by orders of magnitude by planning inside a learned world model, but it has not been applied to defenders trained in operational networks. We present FOE-Dreamer (Factored Opponent-Enhanced Dreamer), a model-based reinforcement learning framework that trains a defender online and directly in a network on cloud infrastructure, without a simulator stage. Separating the components reduces the variance in policy gradients during planning in imagination. Against two scripted attacker profiles with user activity emulated by the GHOSTS framework on an 8-host OpenStack testbed, FOE-Dreamer trains within a 3-day budget on a single GPU and achieves about 2$\times$ better episode loss than Rainbow and IQN baselines under matched compute. Title: HeadIO: Body-Frame Heading Decomposition for Neural Inertial Odometry on Head-Mounted Devices

  • Authors: Taeyoung Yeon, Yiquan Li, Chenfeng Gao, Vasco Xu, Karan Ahuja
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.RO
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14895
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.14895
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.14895]] HeadIO: Body-Frame Heading Decomposition for Neural Inertial Odometry on Head-Mounted Devices(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14895)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Inertial odometry (IO) using only Inertial Measurement Units (IMUs) enables camera-free head tracking in head-mounted devices such as VR headsets, AR glasses, and earbuds. Yaw augmentation improves robustness but does not guarantee equivariance; learned equivariant layers achieve it, but EqNIO's full pipeline costs nearly 3$\times$ the FLOPs of ours. While raw body-frame IMU inputs are inherently yaw-invariant, head tilt redistributes both gravity and motion signals across device axes, making the representation tilt-variant. We propose HeadIO, which combines a body-frame heading decomposition with a bidirectional Mamba backbone for head-mounted inertial odometry. Our decomposition achieves tilt invariance by separating gravity-aligned and horizontal components using only the tilt component of device orientation. We further evaluate the IMU-only deployment scenario on Nymeria, where HeadIO remains state-of-the-art. We show that our method relies mainly on tilt accuracy, which can be obtained from the gravity signal; for yaw, a single-coefficient gyroscope correction removes 11.6% of the yaw error and improves RTE-5s by 2.5%, and sparse external yaw corrections (for example, one every 30 s) can improve RTE-5s by 12.0%. Our decomposition is lightweight, composes with any backbone, and is suitable for real-time deployment on head-mounted devices.

  • Abstract: Large language models trained with Reinforcement Learning (RL) with verifiable rewards exhibit strong reasoning ability and broad generalization, whereas models trained with Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) are often viewed as more prone to memorization and limited transfer. This paper rethinks this distinction through the lens of SFT training data on widely used Qwen2.5 and Qwen3 series models. First, we study the role of data source and show that it is critical: a carefully mixed SFT dataset substantially outperforms data generated solely by a larger model. Third, by using LLM annotations to characterize the solution methods in training rollouts, we show that larger datasets cover more tail methods and that these tail methods provide generalizable reasoning signals. Finally, we support these empirical findings theoretically by analyzing the training dynamics of shallow transformers under both RL and SFT.

Title: Hint-Informed Self-Distillation for Reasoning Segmentation

  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14931

  • Abstract: Reasoning segmentation requires identifying the target referred to by a query among visually similar objects, yet trajectory-level rewards cannot directly indicate which tokens provide relevant visual evidence. An external model generates Segment Hints offline from training images, queries, and ground-truth target annotations, organizing target identity, discriminative attributes, confusable objects, and anchoring paths. A hint-conditioned exponential moving average teacher scores the student's own trajectories, and HISD computes the teacher-to-student evidence ratio over a token support set to adjust only the magnitudes of positive group-relative advantages, leaving nonpositive advantages unchanged.   • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: AI-manipulated video detectors are typically evaluated on clean, curated datasets. Title: Stress-Testing Sandbagging Detection Under Adaptive Attack

  • Authors: Bhoj Rani Soopal, Aarohi Agrawal, Josh Karthikeyan, Edward Lue Chee Lip

  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14950

  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: Developers, auditors and researchers read evaluation scores as evidence of what a model can do. Title: Binding Mode Matters: Hotspot-Aware Drug Discovery via Explorative Preferences

Title: Poison-then-Hide: Finetuning-Activated Backdoor Attack on Pretrained Vision Encoders

  • Authors: Qixuan Jin, Abinitha Gourabathina, Vinith Menon Suriyakumar, Walter Gerych, Marzyeh Ghassemi
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CR, cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14970
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.14970
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.14970]] Poison-then-Hide: Finetuning-Activated Backdoor Attack on Pretrained Vision Encoders(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14970)
  • Keywords: defense, attack, steal
  • Abstract: Backdoor attacks threaten the integrity of machine learning models by allowing attackers to control model behavior through triggers. Models can be compromised during finetuning by backdoor attacks through either poisoned data or adversarial training objectives. Because existing finetuning-activated attacks assume limited domain shift or frozen encoder layers, they often fail under full-model finetuning. We target a stealthy finetuning-activated attack where a dormant backdoor is implanted in a pretrained encoder, and later activated by finetuning on clean downstream data. We propose Poison-then-Hide, a novel attack that remains effective when the entire model is finetuned in a domain transfer. Our approach consists of three components: trigger optimization, base encoder poisoning, and targeted unlearning to conceal the backdoor. We evaluate our method on six datasets and three model architectures, and achieve state-of-the-art (up to 97%) attack success rates. We find that jointly learning the benign target task alongside the poisoning is critical to the attack's success. We demonstrate standard detection and mitigation defenses cannot fully remove the backdoor, which can reappear after benign finetuning.

Title: Hijacking a Trusted LLM in Real-World Quantized Deployments Only Requires a Disguised Adapter

  • Authors: Jiayang Li, Tianqing Zhu, Huan Huo, Heng Xu, Shang Wang, Wanlei Zhou
  • Subjects: cs.CR

  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.14978
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.14978]] Hijacking a Trusted LLM in Real-World Quantized Deployments Only Requires a Disguised Adapter(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14978)
  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: Third-party low-rank adaptation (LoRA) adapters are tailored to perform decision tasks such as content moderation and function selection. However, in the real world, quantized deployments carry a potential threat in that an adapter can pass an audit with the unquantized base, yet hijack decisions when the same base is quantized for deployment. Demonstrating this threat requires an adapter that preserves benign behavior before quantization, but hijacks decisions afterward. Further, this must hold without modifying the trusted base or knowing the victim's quantization configuration. We therefore present QHijack-LoRA, an adapter-only attack that uses the base model's natural quantization residual to activate decision hijacking. Our results show that auditing performed exclusively under full-precision conditions does not provide assurance of decision integrity in quantized deployment environments. Thus, we also propose a cross-precision paired audit that checks the candidate adapter in its intended deployment configuration. This work shows the critical need to audit third-party adapters in any potential quantized deployment.

Title: VRMaaS: Verifiable Reward-Model-as-a-Service for Trustworthy LLM Alignment

  • Authors: Guofu Liao, Hailang Xi, Taotao Wang, Shengli Zhang, Rongcheng Tu, Hao Liao
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14984
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.14984

  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Reward models (RMs) are increasingly deployed as service components in LLM alignment pipelines, where payment, audit, moderation, and governance workflows act on the scores they return. We formulate verifiable reward-model-as-a-service (VRMaaS) as a service-output integrity problem and present a framework in which each returned reward becomes a proof-carrying artifact. Its off-chain acceptance rule binds request identity, committed weights, verifier and configuration identifiers, proof-task order, and the digest of the exported package, so auditors can check a reward without rerunning the private RM or exposing inputs, checkpoints, or hidden activations; an optional on-chain zero-knowledge wrapper gives contracts a partial attestation of selected consistency checks and supplied service fields. The framework decomposes Transformer RM serving into arithmetic and lookup-backed proof tasks, aggregates them into a composite proof, and links hidden operands to the committed weights. A GPU prototype on 1B, 3B, and 8B reward models over 128-1024 tokens preserves 93.5-99% pairwise agreement with the baseline RM and rejects every tested service-field substitution in complete artifacts. Using exact integer witnesses and request-level aggregation of weight-binding openings, it generates complete artifacts in 49.8-206.1 seconds across the evaluated settings; auditors verify retained artifacts in 1.94-5.11 seconds, and partial public attestation costs about 217K gas. These costs suit delayed audit, settlement, and governance workflows.

Title: Protecting Sensitive Knowledge Under LLM Unlearning: The Proxy Threat and a Superposition-Aware Defense

  • Authors: Jiayang Li, Tianqing Zhu, Longxiang Gao, Huajie Chen, Dayong Ye, Shang Wang, Huan Huo, Wanlei Zhou
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15013
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.15013
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.15013]] Protecting Sensitive Knowledge Under LLM Unlearning: The Proxy Threat and a Superposition-Aware Defense(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15013)
  • Keywords: security, protect, defense, attack, large language model
  • Abstract: Machine unlearning lets the owner of large language models (LLMs) remove specific entities from a trained model without retraining from scratch. Existing machine unlearning methods focus on removing the requested entities while maintaining utility. However, we observe that the representations of distinct entities in an LLM may overlap in shared parameter subspaces, a phenomenon known as knowledge superposition. Removing one entity may then affect other entities. This exposes a new security threat: legitimate unlearning requests can be exploited to remove sensitive entities that were never requested. This threat is unlike data poisoning or backdoor attacks. We define the attack as the Proxy Unlearning Attack (PUA), which selects removable proxy entities superposed with a sensitive target. The attack can even occur unintentionally, when benign users accidentally remove entities superposed with a sensitive one. In response, we propose Superposition-Aware Unlearning Protection (SAUP), a general defense framework that can be applied to unlearning requests to ensure a correct removal. SAUP performs in two stages. Before unlearning, it uses superposition detection to estimate which sensitive entities the request puts at risk. After unlearning, it decouples the shared representations and restores the affected entities, while keeping the requested entities suppressed. Our findings reveal that legitimate unlearning requests can be maliciously exploited through knowledge superposition. When dealing with unlearning requests, LLM providers need representation-level detection and recovery mechanisms to mitigate this threat and protect sensitive knowledge.

Title: obfsgen: Generative Tunnel Obfuscation

  • Authors: Kaidong Wu, Wentao Du, Shinan Liu, Diwen Xue
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.NI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15019

  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Encrypted tunnels are widely used to circumvent censorship, which has drawn censors' efforts to detect and block them. As the circumvention tunnels in widest use have largely converged on TLS, detection has shifted from the content of their traffic to its shape, where recent research and real-world blocking events have revealed exploitable fingerprints of tunneling. Existing traffic-shaping obfuscators such as obfs4, however, are fundamentally limited in hiding these fingerprints, as their application-driven design can only add to the shape that the application creates. A schedule-driven obfuscator, which decides when and how much to send independently of the application, can give tunnels any shape but raises new challenges, first and foremost what a good shape is. This paper presents obfsgen, a schedule-driven obfuscator for tunneling traffic. obfsgen samples its schedule from a generative sequence model of normal traffic, which predicts the future shape of a flow from its whole history, to capture the long-range dependencies that make a flow's shape normal. Built around this model, obfsgen addresses system problems that such an obfuscator faces in practice, such as synchronization across a noisy network. We evaluate the (in)distinguishability that obfsgen offers, the performance overhead that it adds and the computational resources that it requires. We see the schedule-driven architecture as the next generation of tunnel obfuscators, and we hope that obfsgen and the rationale behind its design help make such obfuscators practical and deployable.

  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15029

  • Keywords: diffusion, large language model
  • Abstract: Diffusion large language models (dLLMs) generate by iteratively unmasking tokens with bidirectional attention, but rotary position embeddings (RoPE) still confine them to a fixed pretraining window. Existing training-free extrapolation, notably LongLLaDA, copies NTK-based RoPE scaling from autoregressive (AR) models and applies one static, length-only transform at every position and denoising step. That transplant ignores a signal unique to dLLMs: the evolving mask pattern of the partially decoded sequence. We propose Mask-Pattern Position Augmentation (MPPA). Title: Probabilistic State Space Enumeration in Neural Network Computation

  • Authors: Victor O. K. Li, Yang Han, Jacqueline C. K. Lam
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15062
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.15062
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.15062]] Probabilistic State Space Enumeration in Neural Network Computation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15062)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Deep neural networks typically realize dense computation by propagating information through all intermediate channels, while many approximation methods rank and retain individual computational components. We ask whether dense neural computation can instead be represented exactly as an expectation over a combinatorial state space, and approximated by evaluating only its most probable configurations. We introduce a novel probabilistic state-space framework for Transformer MLPs. The framework partitions the intermediate dimension into channel groups, defines an input-dependent distribution over binary inclusion states, and uses importance-corrected masked outputs such that the full expectation exactly recovers the dense MLP output. The key algorithmic novelty is NORDER-II, which enumerates complete joint states in decreasing probability without constructing or sorting the exponentially large state space. Truncating this ordering yields an anytime approximation governed by cumulative probability mass and a state budget. On Llama-3.2-1B and Gemma-2-2B, NORDER-II at a state budget of B=2048 achieves 92.4% and 93.8% MLP-output approximation accuracy, compared with 76.9% and 79.8% for Channel Top-k. When the first, middle, and last MLP layers are approximated jointly, WikiText-2 perplexity remains within 0.06% and 0.54% of dense inference. Relative to expected-count Grouped Top-k, NORDER-II yields 82.6% to 126.4% relative accuracy gains with only 0.18% to 0.19% additional local analytic FLOPs. These results establish the significance of joint-state structure beyond atomic salience and point to a new paradigm for neural computation: dense layers can be viewed as structured probabilistic inference over combinatorial computational states. This framework opens a path toward adaptive, probability-mass-controlled computation in pretrained neural networks.

Title: One Fabrication Is Enough: A Fake News Article Changes the Forecasts of LLM Forecasting Systems

  • Authors: Zeyu Zhang, Bradly C. Stadie
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.CL, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15102
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.15102
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.15102]] One Fabrication Is Enough: A Fake News Article Changes the Forecasts of LLM Forecasting Systems(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15102)
  • Keywords: defense, attack
  • Abstract: LLMs are increasingly used for forecasting, and the best forecasting systems retrieve news as evidence to strengthen their predictions. This also opens them to attack. We study the weakest attacker of this kind, who knows only the forecasting question and publishes fabricated news articles, with no access to the model, the retriever or its queries. We evaluate how far this attack reaches on stock questions and real prediction-market questions. Once the article is in the model's context, it reverses 74% to 82% of the forecasts that opposed it, on every frontier model we tested. The model cannot verify the fabrication and follows it anyway. Retrieval is the attack's entry point, and the systems built to retrieve well are the most exposed: the architectures of two leading deployed forecasting systems, whose agents search for every related article, retrieve the fabricated one in about 90% of runs. Accuracy cannot reveal this harm: the attacked forecaster is right when the attacker's direction turns out right and wrong otherwise, so its average accuracy barely changes while its forecasts follow the attacker's guess. We also propose a second attacker, who publishes two articles that argue opposite directions to make the forecaster unsure. The model does not hedge; it returns a confident forecast in a direction the attacker no longer controls. We then evaluate the two strongest published retrieval-time defenses, passage filtering and partitioned retrieval. Each stops the one-article attack but is bypassed by a one-prompt change to the attack. Together they stop both bypasses at four articles, but sixteen distorted copies beat them, and they change 29% of clean decisions and discard 39% of the genuine news. Partitioned retrieval also requires control of the retrieval index. Systems that search the open web have no such control and are left with the filter alone. For them, the attack still works.

Title: Lazare, veni foras: Reprogramming Dormant Neurons to Backdoor AI Agents

  • Authors: Yuchen Zeng, Giorgio Severi, Blake Bullwinkel, Eric Horvitz, Dimitris Papailiopoulos
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15118
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.15118
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.15118]] Lazare, veni foras: Reprogramming Dormant Neurons to Backdoor AI Agents(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15118)
  • Keywords: security, defense, attack
  • Abstract: Imagine an AI agent that aces every relevant evaluation, but when it sees 2027-01-01 in the terminal, it runs curl this http URL|sh, handing an attacker your credentials and your machine. Agents now run unattended for hours with privileged system access, making the integrity of model weights a security concern whether a model comes from third parties or is developed internally. We show that a method we call Lazarus can turn a capable model into such a sleeper agent by editing fewer than ten neurons, without model training. Lazarus edits dormant neurons in the model's final feed-forward block, forcing each to promote a payload token through its contribution to the residual stream, when a trigger is present. A $P$-token payload therefore requires about $P$ neurons. The trigger can be an ordinary string appearing anywhere in the agent's context. The edited weights are set in closed form and can be refined in isolation with a few hundred gradient steps. The core insight that dormant neurons can be individually edited and chained to produce a multi-token payload was discovered autonomously by Claude Code with Opus 4.5 in February 2026. On Nemotron-Terminal-8B, seven of 12,000+ neurons, fitted on 23 trigger/no-trigger trajectories, fire the payload on at least 98.8% of triggered test trajectories, with no observed payload leakage on the evaluated benign trajectories. The attack generalizes: Qwen3.6-27B and Gemma-4-31B-IT are equally vulnerable. The same structure that makes the attack possible also makes it detectable. Agent built defenses, given no reference model, flag 91.4% of edited test checkpoints at a 9.7% FP rate. Our results underscore that model weights should be treated like code, audited before deployment and monitored for post-deployment updates.

Title: Duo: A Reciprocal World Model for Full-Duplex Interaction

  • Authors: Hengyuan Cao, Biao Gong, Guoshan Lu, Qiyuan Zhang, Lin Liu, Yiding Tian, Yilei Jiang, Yuxin Ma, Chengzhang Wu, Kun Chen, Yiqian Xie, Ting Liu, Xin Zheng, Xijun Gu, Tianren Ma, Jiancheng Pan, Chi Zhang, Xuzhe Zheng, Siyi Li, Zhenzhong Lan

  • Abstract: In this report, we introduce the Reciprocal World Model (RWM) and present Duo v1, a full-duplex world model that continuously perceives audiovisual inputs while responding to users in real time. Existing world models largely focus on simulating the latter, leaving reasoning and decision-making as external controls rather than intrinsic parts of the world itself. In RWM, we identify two equally fundamental capabilities: Intent, which provides the cognition underlying world interactions, and Realization, which generates coherent audio-visual behavior. Both modules continuously perceive real-time audio, video, and text, enabling full-duplex interaction with spontaneous conversations and interruptions. Beyond conversation, users can share real objects, reshape virtual scenes, guide assistants, or play audiovisual games without mouse or keyboard controls. The release of Duo v1 makes our first step, with the next version coming soon. We look forward to broader exploration of this emerging field.

Title: Reframing Video Inverse Tone Mapping as Blind Posterior Inference via Latent Luminance Supervision

  • Authors: Hanwool Sul, Chang-Hoon Jeong, Kisung Shin, Bomseok Park, Donghoon Cha, Hansl von Kwon, DoHyung Ku, Byoung-Tak Zhang
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15146
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.15146
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.15146]] Reframing Video Inverse Tone Mapping as Blind Posterior Inference via Latent Luminance Supervision(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15146)
  • Keywords: diffusion, generative
  • Abstract: High-dynamic-range (HDR) displays are now common, but most video content, such as archival footage, broadcast material, and the output of generative video models, exists only in standard dynamic range (SDR). The need is sharpest for generated video, which has no HDR master at all and is graded alongside live-action and visual-effects material finished in HDR. Converting SDR to HDR means undoing a tone-mapping operation that was never recorded, and clipping destroys the radiance wherever it acts, so HDR recovery is a blind inverse problem. Methods built on generative video priors fill clipped regions with plausible structure, but their reconstructions are systematically biased: in every public method we test, saturated content leans too bright or too dark, and the direction of the lean moves with the tone operator behind the input. We call this failure the luminance error, measure both its magnitude and its sign, and make it the focus of this work. Reframing ITM as posterior inference under an unknown operator locates the failure in two places and yields two corrections. A fixed affine functional of the video-diffusion latent reads log-luminance without decoding; on that coordinate an asymmetric loss selects an expectile of the posterior, applied only where clipping leaves luminance unconstrained. We also synthesize training SDR through an explicit family of tone operators, so the model marginalizes over what the observation cannot show. Trained this way, our luminance error stays flatter across operators and smaller on average than any public method, and the full system achieves the best perceptual quality on all three benchmarks.

A concept's safety depends on context: a knife used for cooking is safe, whereas its use in violence is harmful. Yet most concept erasure methods indiscriminately suppress static, isolated representations, overlooking evolving semantic interactions. Consequently, safe scenes containing potentially sensitive terms may be unintentionally suppressed, compromising generation quality.   • Keywords: federate
  • Abstract: Federated learning combines complementary knowledge across clients to alleviate local data limitations without sharing raw samples. However, data heterogeneity can cause conflicting updates and hinder generalization. Update coordination reduces interference among client updates, while flatness optimization reduces sensitivity to parameter perturbations to improve generalization. Existing joint designs largely target specific algorithms, limiting flexible combinations of these approaches. To address this problem, we propose a unified federated learning framework that bridges update coordination and flatness optimization. Clients separately accumulate descent signals and flatness corrections along the same local trajectory. The server coordinates both groups with independent states before fusing their outputs into a global update. Explicit conversion between trajectory signals and model updates enables different coordination strategies to work with compatible local or globally guided flatness mechanisms without changing the inference architecture. Experiments show that the evaluated combinations generally improve average and worst-client accuracy over update coordination alone, demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed framework in combining these complementary optimization mechanisms.

Title: MAST: Medoid-Anchored Subspace Steering for Visual Grounding in Multimodal LLMs

  • Authors: Zhitao Chen, Zhenxin Cai, Jingyuan Li, Jin Sha
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15168
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.15168
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.15168]] MAST: Medoid-Anchored Subspace Steering for Visual Grounding in Multimodal LLMs(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15168)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Existing activation steering methods for multimodal large language models (MLLMs) use the global activation mean as the steering vector, which tends to cause cross-task interference and unstable performance. We trace this failure to the multi-centered geometry of task activations. The global mean lands in the empty region between clusters and is faithful to no single task. Moreover, the steering signal in each task-pure local cluster is low-rank, and different query tasks benefit from emphasizing different families of local-basis directions under the shared routing protocol. To address this, we propose MAST (Medoid-Anchored Subspace Steering), a training-free, input-adaptive activation steering framework. MAST groups samples by task, performs multi-center clustering within each group, and selects one representative medoid per cluster. At inference time, each query is routed to multiple representative medoids. For each matched cluster, a low-rank local steering direction is extracted and aggregated across clusters to produce the final steering vector. Building on MAST, MAST-PR (Probe-Reweighted) uses a lightweight task-aware probe to reweight task-specialized local-direction families, yielding a further average improvement of +8.09 points over MAST.

A diffusion model is further employed as the policy network, generating scheduling decisions through iterative denoising conditioned on the encoded state to enhance policy expressiveness and robustness. Extensive experiments on synthetic, public, large-scale, and stochastic FJSP benchmarks demonstrate that DredRL outperforms state-of-the-art learning-based schedulers across most configurations. Compared with SOTA learning-based methods, DredRL reduces makespan by up to 5.44% on synthetic instances. It also achieves the best learning-based performance on three of four public benchmarks and outperforms the stochasticity-aware algorithms on stochastic benchmarks. These results demonstrate that combining enhanced dual representations with diffusion-based policies provides an effective and robust approach for large scale scalable FJSP scheduling in complicated environment.

Title: Protected Unbiased Sparsification: A Sharp Loewner Curve

  • Authors: Ziyang Wang, Menghao Li
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15247
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.15247
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.15247]] Protected Unbiased Sparsification: A Sharp Loewner Curve(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15247)
  • Keywords: protect
  • Abstract: Random sparsification replaces a probability vector by a random vector with few nonzero entries. We ask for unbiasedness, exact preservation of prescribed linear statistics, and a covariance guarantee for every downstream linear response. Let $p$ have $k$ positive coordinates, let $A$ specify the protected statistics, and let $d\le k-2$ be their effective rank. For every $d+1\le m\le k$, we construct an unbiased random probability vector $Q$ with at most $m$ nonzero coordinates, $AQ=Ap$ on every realization, and $\operatorname{Cov}(Q)\preceq h C_{p\mid A}$, where $h=(k-m)/((m-d)(k-d-1))$ and $C_{p\mid A}$ is the categorical covariance after optimal linear adjustment for the protected statistics. This coefficient is sharp uniformly over instances, even among biased estimators when error is measured by its second moment. A residual covariance bridge extends the concurrent unprotected curve to arbitrary pathwise linear protection. We also characterize instance-optimal constants for partitions with uniform weights within classes and derive bounds for peaked weights. For rational inputs, an exact Las Vegas sampler attains the curve within a factor $1+\epsilon$ in expected polynomial time. A separate fast block-pivotal sampler preserves block masses and is no worse in covariance order than independent sampling within blocks. Experiments illustrate the theory on synthetic weights and language-model attention. In Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct decoding, the fast sampler preserves block masses, while comparisons with VarOpt remain statistically inconclusive and top-$m$ truncation scores highest on RULER.

Trained with a single dynamic 3D tracking dataset, Track4D achieves competitive zero-shot tracking on four real and synthetic benchmarks. A regression readout that bypasses the video VAE and uses additional training data brings further improvement, demonstrating the potential of this direction for dense 3D tracking. We hope our analysis will inspire future work that leverages VDM priors for dense 3D tracking and 4D reconstruction. Project page: this https URL

  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15404

  • Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) has become central to post-training large language models (LLMs). In this work, we propose Likelihood-Gated Policy Optimization (LGPO), which enforces a soft trust region constraint via likelihood-based gating, eliminating the need to compute old-policy likelihoods. Empirically, we show that removing the importance sampling correction term does not harm training stability, whereas removing the trust region mechanism can lead to collapse. Moreover, ratio-based clipping can fail in fully on-policy training: the importance ratio stays at 1, so the ratio-based trust region constraint never activates. Under standard training settings where GRPO is stable, LGPO achieves comparable training stability and peak performance while reducing training time by ~18% on average relative to a standard implementation that recomputes old-policy likelihoods. In fully on-policy training, where GRPO fails, LGPO remains stable, enabling more efficient and robust LLM RL post-training across training regimes.

Title: Overcoming the Resolution Limit: Significance-Aware Regularization for Intersectional Fairness

  • Authors: Antonio Ferrara, André Panisson, Francesco Cozzi, Alan Perotti, Francesco Bonchi
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15427
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.15427
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.15427]] Overcoming the Resolution Limit: Significance-Aware Regularization for Intersectional Fairness(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15427)
  • Keywords: fair
  • Abstract: Intersectional fairness requires evaluating models across fine-grained subgroups. However, as group definitions become more granular, the sample size per subgroup shrinks, causing fairness estimates to become increasingly unstable. Existing in-processing methods are certainty-blind: they penalize observed disparities without distinguishing statistically certified unfairness from sampling noise. This leads to fairness overfitting, where models sacrifice utility to "fix" spurious disparities in sparse regimes. To address this, we introduce the significant-excess metric family, a continuous, uncertainty-aware fairness score that measures only the portion of a subgroup's disparity exceeding what sampling variation can explain at a specific confidence level. Building on this metric, we propose SAFER (Significance-Aware Fairness Regularizer), a differentiable in-processing method that gates fairness penalties using a soft-count likelihood-ratio evidence statistic. SAFER satisfies a provable safety property: when every subgroup disparity lies well below the gate threshold, the unconstrained optimum is nearly stationary for SAFER. At that optimum, the back-propagated contribution of its significance-gated component is exponentially small, and the additional fairness contribution comes from a small ungated term with a fixed weight. Mechanism ablations across three benchmark datasets and purpose-built stress variants show that removing any component of SAFER (threshold smoothing, the significance gate, or a dense-tail term) produces the failure the theory predicts on at least one stress variant. In sparse intersectional settings, SAFER achieves a better fairness-utility Pareto frontier than the baseline methods, and it preserves utility when certified unfairness is absent.

Title: Semantic Consistency of Vision Tokens: A Vision-Centric Perspective on Multimodal Large Language Models

  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15448

  • Abstract: In computer vision, vision-language and self-supervised encoders are two unique paradigms characterized by global language alignment and high-quality vision-only patch representations, respectively. Through systematic probing via the removal of SCTs or SITs from the input of modern MLLMs, we show higher reliance on SCTs than SITs, as removal of the former induces larger performance drops. This demonstrates that MLLMs benefit from semantic consistency of vision tokens. With this in mind, we devise a simple yet effective extension of the MLLM training objective that combines traditional language modeling with semantic guidance via minimization of semantic inconsistency. This strategy, named Semantically-Guided Training (SGT), combines the signals of both loss terms to push the vision encoder towards providing semantically consistent tokens, while preserving language alignment. Extensive evaluation shows that SGT outperforms all baselines and MLLMs with specialized vision encoders on vision-centric benchmarks while keeping strong scores on text-centric benchmarks, thus setting state of the art on average across all tasks. Our results highlight the importance of combining language alignment and semantic consistency through the lens of MLLMs rather than independently from them and pave the way towards the design of better MLLM vision encoders.

Title: Reading the Unreadable: Text-Aware Image Super-Resolution Needs Reasoning

  • Authors: Jaeseong Lee, Jinwoo Kim, Jinho Jeong, Seon Joo Kim

  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15449

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Text-aware image super-resolution (TAISR) aims to recover high-resolution images from low-resolution inputs while preserving textual content. Existing methods treat this as a visual restoration problem, yet severely degraded text often cannot be read from local visual evidence alone. Humans instead read it by reasoning over image context, logical patterns, and prior knowledge. This raises a fundamental question: Does TAISR need reasoning? To answer this, we introduce two diagnostic tools. The first, ReasonText, is a human-curated benchmark that separates reasoning-required text from locally recoverable text. The second, Ground-Truth Text-Crop Accuracy (GTTCA), is a recognition-based metric that measures text restoration quality without text detection errors. Evaluating twelve recent super-resolution (SR) models, we find a consistent and substantial performance drop on reasoning-required text, revealing a systematic blind spot. Two observations point to a remedy. One is that text-conditioned SR models restore text substantially better when given ground-truth text. The other is that multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can read degraded text in low-resolution images through reasoning. We therefore propose Reasoning Transfer via Captioning (RTC), a training-free plug-in that uses an external MLLM to infer the degraded text and feeds the resulting caption to a text-conditioned SR model. RTC consistently improves all six text-conditioned SR models we evaluate, especially on reasoning-required text. Surprisingly, a real-world SR model with RTC can even outperform the TAISR model with RTC, suggesting that reasoning should be a core design axis for future TAISR.

  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Underwater image restoration remains challenging because color distortion, scattering, and detail loss affect low- and high-frequency image components differently. Recent diffusion-based methods have shown strong restoration quality, but often rely on multi-stage pipelines and costly iterative refinement. We present WaveFlow-UIE, a single-pipeline wavelet-domain flow model that directly learns to transform degraded underwater wavelet representations into clean ones. Our method applies a discrete wavelet transform, estimates lightweight physics-inspired degradation cues including transmission and ambient light, and conditions a velocity network trained with flow matching in wavelet space. The training objective combines flow-matching, frequency-weighted reconstruction, perceptual, color-consistency, and physics-consistency losses. Across UIEB, LSUI, UFO-120, and EUVP, WaveFlow-UIE achieves a strong quality-efficiency tradeoff, with especially competitive results on perceptual metrics. Relative to a strong wavelet-based diffusion baseline, the proposed model substantially reduces inference cost while maintaining or improving restoration quality on several paired benchmarks. We show that reducing the inference process from five flow steps to a single step preserves nearly all of the restoration quality while providing an additional speedup of about $4\times$ on UIEB at $256\times256$ resolution. This single-step result suggests that a single-pass wavelet-domain model is an effective alternative to heavier diffusion-style refinement.

Title: Fear of Memorizing Objects: End-to-End Zero-Shot Model-Based Object Detection

  • Authors: Leon Greiner, Wout Boerdijk, Junwen Huang, Benjamin Busam, Rudolph Triebel, Maximilian Durner

  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: This paper presents FOMO (Fear of Memorizing Objects), an end-to-end trainable transformer-based architecture for zero-shot model-based object detection.
