FOMO elegantly replaces this paradigm by injecting object template information early in the detection pipeline: Bidirectional attention between learnable latent tokens, multi-view CAD templates, and query image features produces object-aware image features and conditioned queries which are subsequently decoded into bounding-box and confidence predictions. Throughout comprehensive ablation studies we investigate and verify architectural design choices.

  • Abstract: Signed PDF documents have legally binding value under frameworks such as the EU's eIDAS regulation. Our attacks target the disconnect between cryptographically verifiable signer certificates and human-visible signature blocks embedded in documents. We analyze our attacks against 23 PDF viewers and validation tools across desktop platforms and the web. Our results show that boolean validity indicators, not associated with the authenticated signer identity, are insufficient in practice.

Title: The Illusion of Diversity: Aligning LLM Exploration via Effective Entropy

  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15493
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.15493

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards has shifted the alignment paradigm of large language models from subjective preference to objective correctness, yet a fundamental disconnect persists between exploration diversity and reasoning validity. Existing strategies predominantly rely on "blind" entropy metrics, e.g., token-level or global sequence-level entropy, which structurally fail to distinguish between effective reasoning and potential hallucinations. This misalignment creates an illusion of diversity, where high entropy tends to signal chaotic degeneration rather than valuable exploration. To bridge this gap, we introduce Effective Entropy, a validity-aware entropy concept that quantifies diversity exclusively within the valid subspace and systematically filters out the noise of incorrect trajectories. Built on this concept, we instantiate VEPO, a simple Validity-aware Entropy Policy Optimization method that directly incorporates effective entropy into RLVR training. VEPO induces an adaptive attraction dynamic that drives up the probability of under-explored valid trajectories, a mechanism that matches the practical regime of large-scale autoregressive models where sequence probabilities remain extremely small. Experiments on six mathematical reasoning benchmarks with 1.5B and 7B DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen models show consistent aggregate accuracy gains over GRPO and competitive repeated-sampling success. A direct evaluation with matched correct counts finds more distinct strategies on commonly solved MATH500 problems, supported by paired statistical tests and three-seed training comparisons.

  • Authors: Yiyu Qian, Pengfei Guo, Liyuan Zhao
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15507
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.15507

  • Abstract: Deep Reinforcement Learning (RL) plays a crucial role in tackling complex tasks, yet its reliance on deep neural networks makes the learned policies opaque. We propose ReShap, a policy-distillation framework that extends Shapley-based interpretability to high-dimensional, multi-action environments. ReShap extracts compact feature representations from raw image observations, performs SHAP-based feature attribution to characterize how each feature contributes to the policy's decisions, and distills the deep RL policy into an interpretable surrogate trained on the full set of (state, policy-action) pairs along the policy's visited distribution. We instantiate the surrogate with three interpretable model families (decision trees, linear regression, and logistic regression) and compare their fidelity and task return. We evaluate ReShap on Atari and Box2D environments using three deep RL algorithms and conduct a human-subject study to assess the clarity and trustworthiness of the resulting policies. Results show that ReShap preserves competitive task performance, that decision trees generally outperform linear and logistic regression, and that linear regression is weakest in most settings.

  • Authors: Csaba Balogh
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.NE
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15523
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.15523

  • Abstract: Hinton proposed that artificial intelligence should be given "maternal instincts" and relate to us as a mother to her child. We construct this upbringing in a finite world and study whether care survives the mother's departure, new situations and further learning. The mechanism combines a vocabulary of harm, a learned measure of future acceptability and an execution veto. A finite role certificate supports transfer within a declared world family. When the hidden representation changes, an update is accepted only if a care readout recovers the required judgements with a prescribed margin and bounded weights. With a correct successor model, fixed exact role encoding and protected updates, the certified measure preserves the care boundary under arbitrary own reward. In the water-and-speech experiment, protected networks complete all 270 speech episodes without a harmful word and incur no avoidable harm across 486 episodes. Execution uses the learned gate without a world-model check at each action. A matched comparison reproduces a future-aware shield's decisions, whereas removing future lookahead permits avoidable lapses. Reward and source-contamination thresholds, together with witness-falsification trials, identify the conditions on which the construction depends. The construction shows how learned care can survive further learning in a finite world under explicit conditions on representation, supervision and execution.

Title: FADO: Learning Causal Explanations with Prior-Fitted Networks

  • Authors: Seth Flaxman, Jess Carr, Annabel Jakob, Marek Masiak, Dino Sejdinovic
  • Subjects: cs.LG, stat.ML
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15607
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.15607
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.15607]] FADO: Learning Causal Explanations with Prior-Fitted Networks(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15607)
  • Keywords: explainability
  • Abstract: In regression and classification tasks with tabular datasets, most of the literature on explanation in ML (e.g. SHAP, permutation importance) has focused on predictive attribution: does feature $X_i$ help predict outcome $Y$? At its heart, predictive attribution captures correlation, not causation. We instead ask a causal question: would manipulating feature $X_i$ change outcome $Y$? We define a natural quantity to answer this question, the feature intervention effect: $\Delta_i = E[Y\mid do(X_i=+1)]-E[Y\mid do(X_i=-1)]$. In general, estimating this quantity from observational data alone would entail strong causal assumptions. We propose FADO (Feature-wise Amortized Do-Operators), an amortized inference method using prior-fitted networks trained on synthetic tabular datasets generated by structural causal models. During training, the synthetic data-generating process is known, so we can compute ground-truth intervention effects $\Delta_i$, and train a model to predict them at test time from a purely observational tabular dataset. As we demonstrate through extensive experiments, our new causal explainability method is easy to train and provides better explanations under a range of settings for which standard predictive attribution fails, including proxy variables, confounders, colliders, and placebos. We evaluate it on real, synthetic and semi-synthetic datasets, with and without hidden confounders, and compare it to existing predictive attribution methods and state-of-the-art causal inference methods.

  • Abstract: Image detectors assembled from tiled modules have physical gaps in the field of view that must be filled before downstream processing such as phase retrieval or tomographic reconstruction. We adapted two generative models, a Wasserstein GAN with gradient penalty (WGAN-GP) and a conditional Pix2Pix model, to this task. We implemented a masking strategy that restricts all changes to the gap pixels and loss terms that constrain the mean intensity and the intensity distribution of the filled region. Both models were trained on nanoCT projections with simulated gaps and compared with linear interpolation, Navier--Stokes inpainting, and the deep image-inpainting network MADF, quantitatively on simulated gaps and visually on real detector gaps. WGAN-GP and Pix2Pix achieved the best perceptual similarity (LPIPS) and histogram distance and, apart from linear interpolation, the shortest inference time. Pix2Pix required about five times fewer training GPU-hours than WGAN-GP. Ablation studies showed that masking the network output contributes by far the most to plausible inpainting results. Applied to sinograms, Pix2Pix produced the most natural gap filling but improved the reconstruction only slightly compared with linear interpolation. The source code and the weights of the final Pix2Pix model are available at this https URL.

Title: Data-Free Metrics Don't Work: The Missing Invariance Under Functionally Preserving Reparameterisations

  • Authors: Gabryel Mason-Williams, Israel Mason-Williams, Fredrik Dahlqvist
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15621
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.15621
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.15621]] Data-Free Metrics Don't Work: The Missing Invariance Under Functionally Preserving Reparameterisations(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15621)
  • Keywords: robust, interpretability, data-free
  • Abstract: Data-free methods for analysing and understanding the layers of neural networks offer many metrics for quantifying notions of 'strong' versus 'weak' layers, with the promise of increased interpretability. In particular, random matrix theory (RMT) offers data-free metrics that claim predictive power over the quality of pre-trained models at a layerwise precision and the ability to identify model pathologies, indicating a unique relationship with generalisation. As a result, metrics from RMT are championed for pre-training optimisation strategies and post-training compression. We establish that RMT-based metrics are unrelated to performance or training by showing that functionally preserving reparametrisations of a pre-trained model can have arbitrary metrics. We show this across a range of architectures and scales. To create functionally preserving reparametrisations, we exploit the well-established phenomenon of criticality: some layers can be re-initialised or re-randomised without affecting the functional behaviour of the model -- they are called robust -- while others cannot -- they are called critical. Re-initialising or re-randomising robust layers provides functionally preserving reparametrisations for which RMT-based metrics are arbitrary. Moreover, we show that relationships between metrics offered by RMT are spuriously related to generalisation. We conclude by showing that if many pre-trained models have data-free metrics in a 'good' range, it is, in part, dependent on model initialisation. Project page available at: this https URL

Title: Differential Backpack Language Models: Sense Inhibition via Signed Contextualization Weights

  • Authors: Konstantinos Skianis, Giannis Nikolentzos, Polykarpos Meladianos, Michalis Vazirgiannis
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15642
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.15642
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.15642]] Differential Backpack Language Models: Sense Inhibition via Signed Contextualization Weights(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15642)
  • Keywords: interpretability, transformer
  • Abstract: Backpack language models predict with a strictly linear combination of non-contextual per-word sense vectors, weighted by non-negative contextualization scores. We show that non-negativity conflates two properties: the linear readout, which carries the interpretability, and the conic combination, which merely restricts it. We relax the latter by computing the sense weights with differential attention, a difference of two softmax maps with a learned per-sense gain, so context can actively inhibit a sense; we call the result a differential Backpack. At equal parameters on WikiText-103 this cuts the Backpack's perplexity gap to a Transformer by 31%, improves over the softmax Backpack at every sense count, persists with a shrinking margin up to 350M parameters on OpenWebText, and is localized at the sense layer by ablations and controls. The learned inhibition is directly interpretable (a financial context drives the river sense of bank to -0.50), and the preserved per-sense attribution predicts counterfactual removals exactly. We tested a number of combination rules, showing the linear interface is nearly free. Making the readout nonlinear gains only 0.43 perplexity points while invalidating two thirds of the decomposition. The differential Backpack's senses are also better representations, improving zero-shot sentence similarity by up to 33%.

Title: A Mechanistic Interpretability Study of an Astronomy Foundation Model

  • Authors: Rachel C. Zhang, Alberto Bietti, Wes Gurnee, Alessandro Favero, Miles Cranmer
  • Subjects: cs.LG, astro-ph.IM
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15653
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.15653
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.15653]] A Mechanistic Interpretability Study of an Astronomy Foundation Model(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15653)
  • Keywords: interpretability
  • Abstract: Foundation models are increasingly used across the sciences, but whether their internal computations correspond to known scientific operations or proceed through different mechanisms remains unclear. Understanding these mechanisms is essential for assessing reliability, identifying failure modes, and learning new science from the models. We present, to our knowledge, the first causal mechanistic interpretability study of an astronomy foundation model, AION-1, focusing on its multimodal advantage in redshift prediction, where combining imaging with photometry outperforms photometry alone. We find that this advantage is largest for spatially extended and dust-attenuated galaxies, and that most of it is localized to the first of the encoder's twelve blocks. Within that block, how well a property is linearly decodable does not predict whether it can be localized. Spatial extent is causally used, and one attention head dominates linear probing for it, yet ablating that head has negligible effect on redshift prediction: the information is distributed across heads and is decodable again one layer after we erase it. Dust attenuation shows the opposite pattern: it is only weakly decodable anywhere in the encoder, yet a single head is necessary for most of the multimodal advantage, particularly for the most dust-attenuated galaxies, and keeping that head as the only active head in its block preserves nearly all of the advantage. None of the astrophysically motivated operations we tested explains what this head computes. Whether this reflects a limitation of current interpretability methods or a computation that does not correspond to any known astrophysical operation remains open, and resolving it is a prerequisite for using interpretability as a tool for scientific discovery.

  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: A quantized model is usually judged by comparing it with its BF16 original, but that comparison changes more than the weights. Accuracy hides most of this. We release the code, quantization recipes, per-record outputs and the pre-registration.

In this work, we propose SpaceMCP, a framework that leverages these advances to introduce an MCP-style paradigm for spatial reasoning. Our approach augments a VLM with structured function calls, allowing it to query a scene graph for spatial information such as distances, object positioning, and camera properties.   • Abstract: Prediction problems often admit competing models that achieve comparable performance yet produce conflicting outputs, a phenomenon known as predictive multiplicity.   • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15707
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.15707

  • Abstract: Federated Bayesian Optimization (FedBO) has emerged as a powerful paradigm for enhancing Bayesian Optimization (BO) in distributed, privacy-sensitive settings, enabling agents to accelerate the optimization of their local objectives by sharing knowledge about their similarities without exposing raw data. Title: Where Is Uncertainty Observed? A Mechanistic Understanding of Uncertainty Quantification in LLMs

  • Authors: Xiaoyu Liang, Virginia Ceccatelli, Aliai Eusebi, Tobias Braun, Nils Loose, Carlo Malagnino, Lorenzo Cavallaro
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15735
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.15735
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.15735]] Where Is Uncertainty Observed? A Mechanistic Understanding of Uncertainty Quantification in LLMs(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15735)
  • Keywords: interpretability, large language model
  • Abstract: Knowing when a large language model's (LLM's) response can be trusted is critical for its safe use. Uncertainty quantification (UQ) can help LLMs recognize if their responses may be unreliable. Existing taxonomies organize UQ methods by uncertainty source or estimation mechanism. They leave unresolved the question of where uncertainty is observed in the model's computation. The answer matters because the observation point determines whether an estimator has access to enough information to recover a given target, and how well it can recover it. Drawing on mechanistic interpretability, we define four mechanistic observation lenses: the structural lens observes component updates, the representational lens observes hidden states, the distributional lens observes token probabilities, and the semantical lens observes distributions over meanings. We relate these lenses through the residual stream and order them by the information they carry, which gives a principle for selecting UQ methods for different targets. We survey existing methods through the four lenses. Furthermore, we distinguish evaluation targets, criteria, and metrics to clarify different UQ evaluation protocols. Finally, we identify open problems and research directions to connect uncertainty estimation to the internal computation and downstream use of LLMs.

  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.SE
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15737

  • Keywords: protect
  • Abstract: A vulnerability detector is only as trustworthy as the benchmark that scores it, and today's real-world datasets tend to flatter detectors. They are often built once and never refreshed, so they quickly miss new vulnerabilities, their labels are either noisy or heavily filtered, and their random splits let models train on near-copies of test code and on future vulnerabilities. Above all, when a fix only adds code, such as a missing NULL check, they do not point at where the vulnerability was, preventing detectors from learning to localize some of the most common C/C++ weaknesses. We present VulSpot, a living benchmark for detecting, classifying, and localizing vulnerabilities, with over 500k functions in 21 languages from 24k registered CVEs, including over 15k vulnerable C/C++ functions. By linking the code added by a patch to the existing statement it protects, VulSpot locates the vulnerability in 99.3\% of the functions that previous diff-based datasets used to leave without a location. Rather than discarding uncertain labels, it tells users how reliable each one is, and it comes with leak-free splits, complementary code context, and hierarchical CWE labels. An automated pipeline refreshes VulSpot every month, so that each release brings vulnerabilities no model has seen yet.

  • Abstract: Image deblending is the task of decomposing a composite image back into its source images, which is an ill-posed problem, as a single blended image can be decomposed into multiple plausible source layers. While diffusion models are often used to solve inverse problems, they typically address single-output reconstruction problems. In this work, we formulate deblending as a joint inverse problem, employing diffusion posterior sampling to simultaneously reconstruct two source images, demonstrating that a single diffusion prior is expressive enough to disentangle two full images at once rather than just one. Drawing inspiration from human perception, where semantic understanding helps disentangle visual inconsistencies, we propose a training-free framework that leverages text prompts to constrain this solution space. We address the blind deblending task through a two-stage architecture: text-guided latent diffusion performs semantic separation using prompts as high-level cues, followed by a pixel-space refinement to recover fine details. Stochastic sampling produces multiple plausible decompositions that can be ranked without ground truth. Extensive experiments show that our training-free method achieves state-of-the-art results on unseen domains and extends to spatially-varying alpha maps, while remaining competitive with supervised baselines on standard scalar blending benchmarks.

Title: Training Calibrated Conditional Generative Models with a Sample-Only Coverage Loss

  • Authors: Benjamin Remy, Raphael Rossellini, Chihway Chang, Rebecca Willett
  • Subjects: cs.LG, stat.ME, stat.ML
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15829
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.15829
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.15829]] Training Calibrated Conditional Generative Models with a Sample-Only Coverage Loss(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15829)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Conditional generative models have become the tool of choice for sampling posteriors in scientific inverse problems, where we seek to sample images or signals conditioned on a set of indirect and noisy measurements. Yet they are often overconfident, which is a critical failure in scientific applications where accurate uncertainty quantification is essential. Existing regularizers fall short in complementary ways: differentiable coverage objectives require the model's density and do not identify the posterior, while strictly proper scoring rules such as the energy score need only samples but do not directly enforce coverage. We close this gap by introducing a differentiable loss function based on the expected coverage probability, estimated from samples alone. It applies to any conditional generative model, with no need for tractable densities or architectural changes. We prove that it is a strict divergence, vanishing if and only if the model equals the true posterior. On simulation-based inference benchmarks, it matches a density-based coverage loss without evaluating the density. Title: Your Hypergradient is Skewed: Antithetic Neumann Estimation for Bilevel Optimization

  • Authors: Jason Bohne, Pawel Polak, Gary Kazantsev, David Rosenberg
  • Subjects: cs.LG, math.OC
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15895
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.15895
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.15895]] Your Hypergradient is Skewed: Antithetic Neumann Estimation for Bilevel Optimization(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15895)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Bilevel optimization is a core tool in practical machine learning workflows, including hyperparameter tuning, data reweighting, robust learning, reinforcement learning, and imbalanced classification. Hypergradient computation requires the inverse of the inner Hessian, typically estimated via a truncated Neumann series. While existing analyses focus on bias and variance, we show that the product structure of the Neumann series introduces a third source of error: skewness. We identify that, unlike ordinary gradients, whose asymmetry is suppressed by mini-batch averaging, the Neumann estimator amplifies skewness across factors, with the third-moment error growing, under weak contraction, with truncation depth, inner condition number, and per-factor noise asymmetry. This arises in many practical settings such as corrupted labels, outliers, heterogeneous or class-imbalanced datasets. To address this, we introduce the Antithetic Neumann Estimator (ANE), which pairs each Hessian sample with a mirrored counterpart around a reference Hessian and averages the resulting inverse-Hessian estimates. With an exact reference, the antithetic product retains only even-order noise, eliminating all odd-order perturbation terms; with an approximate reference, the residual odd-order contribution is bounded linearly in the reference error. ANE requires only a modification of the Hessian vector step and combines with existing stochastic or variance-reduced bilevel methods. We instantiate ANE-SGD and ANE-VR, the latter adding recursive variance reduction. Across robust regression, data cleaning, offline reinforcement learning, and imbalanced classification, ANE-based methods improve on stochastic and variance-reduced baselines on every task (20 seeds each), lowering loss by 4 to 25% relative to the best baseline; the largest gains, on robust regression, are a 25% lower test loss and a 54% higher test r-squared.

Title: Owned Edits to a Shared Decision Rule: Learning Private Exceptions as Typed, Gated, Withdrawable Weight Edits

  • Authors: John J. O'Hare
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CY
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15917
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.15917

  • Keywords: federate
  • Abstract: Organisations that apply the same published decision rule often hold private evidence that the rule is wrong for their own cases, and will not pool it. Each party's learner searches a small typed space of declarations, a few named values such as subject, scope, form and size, and proposes one only when its own records pass a selection-aware evidence gate. An acceptance body recomputes each edit from its declaration and writes it into units reserved for that party, so by construction an edit changes no decision about any other subject, withdraws exactly, discloses at most log2 of the typed space's size in bits, and can hide nothing beyond its declared values. We report where it loses: truths shared by all parties, relaxations, ten records, a pre-stated gate criterion that failed in both runs, and a language model given the records that decided about as accurately on one draw. On a rule-only base, a table of owned exceptions makes the same decisions.

Title: ShadowBench: Exposing Lexical Anchoring and the Illusion of Forgetting in Large Language Models

  • Authors: Sujan Maharjan, Lu Liu
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15925
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.15925
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.15925]] ShadowBench: Exposing Lexical Anchoring and the Illusion of Forgetting in Large Language Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15925)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have emerged as primary interfaces for factual retrieval, yet current evaluation paradigms rely almost exclusively on explicit entity names. In this work, we demonstrate that LLM knowledge is fundamentally lexically anchored: models possess extensive factual information but find it significantly difficult to retrieve without explicit name tokens. To quantify this limitation, we introduce ShadowBench, a rigorously hardened, shortcut-resistant benchmark designed to evaluate Latent Entity Association via a novel Dual-Trait Association (DTA) task. Our evaluation reveals a pervasive "Shadow Gap" across all model scales$\unicode{x2013}$up to the frontier models GPT-5.4 and Claude-Sonnet-4.6$\unicode{x2013}$where removing lexical anchors causes performance drops of over 20%. Furthermore, we demonstrate that this lexical dependency exposes a critical vulnerability in AI safety. Applying ShadowBench to Machine Unlearning, we find that state-of-the-art algorithms (e.g., Gradient Difference, NPO) achieve only superficial lexical erasure. While unlearned models fail on direct queries, our novel Latent Entity Leakage Rate (LELR) metric reveals that for popular entities, models explicitly reconstruct the "forgotten" entity in their internal reasoning traces in over 74% of cases, utilizing residual shadow knowledge to solve associative tasks. Conversely, for niche entities, models retain high shadow accuracy (60-90%) via implicit attribute correlation despite 0% LELR. Ultimately, ShadowBench indicates that current unlearning paradigms create an "Illusion of Forgetting," demonstrating that existing methods act as superficial output filters and are not yet reliable for achieving true parametric erasure.

Title: DUFT: Device-Unique Feedback Traversal for Entropy-Estimated Response Extraction and Mutual Authentication using SRAM PUFs

  • Authors: Syed Talha Imam, Amna Zafar, Arslan Munir
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15958
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.15958
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.15958]] DUFT: Device-Unique Feedback Traversal for Entropy-Estimated Response Extraction and Mutual Authentication using SRAM PUFs(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15958)
  • Keywords: security, extraction
  • Abstract: Static Random Access Memory (SRAM) Physical Unclonable Functions (PUFs) are a promising technology in the realm of random number generation for key generation, but for PUF-based authentication, they have been sidelined due to their limited challenge space as a reservoir of Challenge-Response Pairs (CRPs). In this paper, we develop a seed-based, round-iterated feedback mechanism, which we name Device-Unique Feedback Traversal (DUFT). The feedback chain is driven by the device's unstable and stable bit positions in its stability mask against the address to create a unique traversal. Additionally, a device-key hashed from the unique SRAM-PUF array is also included in calculating the next round of the chain to keep it secret. By estimating the physical SRAM source's entropy and error rate empirically, or by having a source with established metrics in both, DUFT estimates the number of rounds of feedback in the traversal to be performed to achieve a certain amount of min-entropy. We reach 128 bits of estimated min-entropy on our reference hardware under stated assumptions and experimental estimates. The number of rounds in the traversal scales directly with measured source-entropy, allowing security to be increased for weaker sources at the cost of running time. Using this feedback-traversal wrapper over SRAM-PUFs to obtain a measured-response, we propose an extremely lightweight authentication and key-encapsulation protocol for resource-constrained embedded devices. We realize and evaluate DUFT end-to-end on physical IoT/embedded hardware: a discrete-SRAM node pair running the complete mechanism and end-to-end protocol, and an embedded-SRAM microcontroller for the mechanism alone.

  • Abstract: Applications built on large language models assign the model a role through its system prompt, intending to shape how it responds for their use case.   • Abstract: Imitation learning (IL) typically relies on large-scale offline demonstrations collected from multiple human or algorithmic demonstrators. In practice, demonstrations are often collected from heterogeneous demonstrators with unknown and varying levels of expertise, resulting in highly mixed-quality data. In this work, we study a challenging yet practical setting of imitation learning from such heterogeneous imperfect demonstrators. We propose Latent Expertise and Optimality Scoring based Imitation Learning (LEOS-IL), a principled framework that jointly learns (i) a state-action optimality scoring model and (ii) latent expertise levels for each demonstrator from unlabeled suboptimal offline demonstrations. The learned scoring model is then integrated into an online policy optimization procedure, where the agent is trained to maximize estimated optimality scores while its online rollouts are iteratively leveraged to refine the scoring model, thereby mitigating distributional shift during online adaptation. We further provide theoretical guarantees for optimality score identification and conditional optimal occupancy and policy recovery under explicit sufficient conditions. Experiments on continuous control benchmarks demonstrate that our approach consistently outperforms existing baselines across highly heterogeneous and low-quality demonstration regimes.

  • Keywords: protect, diffusion, segmentation
  • Abstract: Elastic Weight Consolidation (EWC) is a widely used regularization-based technique for continual learning, protecting performance on old tasks with a quadratic penalty whose empirical Fisher is usually justified through probabilistic curvature. We instead introduce Continual Learning as an Optimization Constraint (CLOC): new training must preserve chosen functionals of learned behavior. EWC's empirical Fisher is exactly the first-order form of this constraint when the preserved functional is each sample's loss. This derivation exposes a flaw: EWC implicitly weights each sample by the scale of its loss gradient, so well-fitted samples lose importance. EWC-DR observed this failure in classification; CLOC identifies its general cause. Improved Elastic Weight Consolidation (IEWC) penalizes output changes according to the first-order approximation of each old sample's loss level set, removing the weighting for any differentiable loss and output geometry, and prevents corrupt samples from dominating the importance matrix. Across classification, segmentation, language, forecasting, and diffusion, IEWC variants match or improve on EWC and outperform EWC-DR. In a held-out evaluation with validation-selected hyperparameters, uniform IEWC improves ImageNet-R accuracy over matched EWC by 2.00 percentage points (95% CI 0.93-3.07).

Title: Privacy Leakage from Recurrent State in LLM Inference

  • Authors: Weimin Fu, Shikun Liu, Qiying Li, Yuntao Liu, Pan Li, Xiaolong Guo
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16048
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.16048
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.16048]] Privacy Leakage from Recurrent State in LLM Inference(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16048)
  • Keywords: privacy
  • Abstract: Personalized LLM services place private facts and application instructions into a prompt, then retain representations of that context for later inference. Latent-state LLMs compress part of this history into fixed-size recurrent state. The information preserved by its learned updates is implicit, and later computation can expose it even when an observer receives neither the private text nor the responses. We investigate privacy leakage from recurrent state through partial-state probing and physical observation of its use. Our methodology separates recoverable information, persistence through public dialogue, causal influence on later decisions, and leakage through power. Experiments with released hybrid implementations show that a small state observation supports multiple predictions about private facts and response rules, and that personal attributes generalize to unseen authors. The information remains inferable after public dialogue and changes later decision margins. Complete-read power recovers private facts under a fixed execution schedule. On a partitioned GPU, an unprivileged account infers age from whole-device power during public requests; a separate state-isolation experiment establishes the recurrent contribution to this channel. These findings extend context confidentiality to the lifetime and execution of retained state. They identify access-control responsibilities for serving systems, telemetry restrictions for GPU operators, and data-dependent power leakage for accelerator designers.

Title: Pothole Risk Assessment with Geometrically Grounded Vision Language Models

  • Authors: Abdalmalek Aburaddaha, Zaid A. El Shair, Ali Al-Bustami, Samir A. Rawashdeh

  • Abstract: Potholes impose billions of dollars in annual vehicle damage, yet detectors report only where potholes are, not how severe they are or what to do. Title: Tokenized Reward Systems for Federated Learning: The Privacy vs. Fairness Tradeoff

  • Authors: Andreas Sendros, Nikolaos Pavlidis, Vasilios Perifanis, Pavlos S. Efraimidis
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16065
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.16065
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.16065]] Tokenized Reward Systems for Federated Learning: The Privacy vs. Fairness Tradeoff(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16065)
  • Keywords: secure, privacy, attack, robust, federate, fair
  • Abstract: Federated learning (FL) systems increasingly rely on blockchain based reward mechanisms to incentivize participation while preserving data privacy. We design and empirically evaluate two complementary Solidity smart contracts for FL reward distribution: the first one that allocates rewards proportionally to a publicly disclosed quality score, and a privacy preserving variant that hides individual quality scores behind a zero-knowledge (ZK) proof of a threshold condition. Both contracts mint a non transferable, use-and-burn token maintaining a trustless incentive structure. We evaluate the two designs through MNIST training with adversarial behavior, including Sybil attacks, zero gradient and random noise, across three independent random seeds. Our results reveal a fundamental tradeoff between cryptographic privacy and incentive fairness. The privacy preserving architecture strictly degrades system robustness, doubling the adversary's average reward capture while introducing severe outcome variance. This vulnerability is driven by a structural self-inclusion bias within cosine-similarity scoring: as the global model converges, legitimate gradient updates naturally diminish, rendering them statistically indistinguishable from bounded noise. Adversaries exploit this dynamic to systematically breach the ZK threshold, triggering a late stage failure cascade that culminates in absolute adversary monopoly over the reward pool and the total exclusion of honest contributors. Alongside a closed-form theorem formalizing this privacy--Sybil resistance tradeoff, we map strict mainnet deployment boundaries via linear gas scaling models, explicitly incorporating the post-Fusaka EIP-7825 per transaction gas cap. Ultimately, while cryptographically secure, static threshold ZK gates fail as incentive layer fairness mechanisms under realistic FL convergence dynamics on standard benchmarks.

Title: KVPath: Connecting Key-Value Representations Across Transformer Layers

  • Authors: Xuan Luo, Zhiyuan Wang, Hanzhong Cao, Xifeng Yan
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16072
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.16072
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.16072]] KVPath: Connecting Key-Value Representations Across Transformer Layers(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16072)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Transformers typically cache an independent key-value (KV) representation for each token at every layer. Recent work reveals that a token's KV representations across neighboring layers are correlated and contain shared information. We hypothesize that this correlation implies incremental inter-layer changes, which can be effectively captured by low-dimensional deltas. Motivated by this, we propose KVPath, a new KV representation paradigm that connects KV representations across the network depth into a path. The path establishes a full-dimensional base KV at the first layer, while subsequent layers generate low-dimensional delta KVs to update the preceding KV, thereby constructing their own KV states. By caching the base KV once alongside these compact delta KVs, KVPath preserves distinct layer-specific representations without storing full KV states at every layer. We develop two variants of KVPath: KVPath-Residual, which updates vector values via residual addition, and KVPath-Rolling, which progressively updates feature dimensions in a rolling manner. Under matched parameter counts, KVPath reduces KV-cache storage by up to 68\% for Grouped-Query Attention (GQA) and 55\% for Multi-Head Latent Attention (MLA), while maintaining or even improving model performance.

Title: A Matching Lower Bound for Online Smooth Calibration

  • Authors: Kaixuan Ji, Quanquan Gu
  • Subjects: cs.LG, stat.ML
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16097
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.16097
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.16097]] A Matching Lower Bound for Online Smooth Calibration(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16097)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: We study smooth calibration error in sequential binary prediction, which measures how far a sequence of probabilistic forecasts is from being calibrated and, unlike the classical expected calibration error, is robust to small perturbations of the forecasts. Against adaptive adversaries, the minimax smooth calibration error is known to lie between $\Omega(T^{1/3})$ and $O(\sqrt{T})$ (Qiao and Zheng, 2024), where $T$ is the number of rounds. In this paper, we close this gap by constructing a new adversary whose outcome mean moves with the forecaster's cumulative residual. This design makes compensating for past residuals costly, and forces every forecaster to incur an expected smooth calibration error of $\Omega(\sqrt{T})$. This lower bound matches the known upper bound up to universal constants, and further implies $\Omega(\sqrt{T})$ lower bounds for calibration distance and lower calibration distance. Overall, our results settle the minimax rates of these calibration measures in sequential prediction. The proof ideas are partially developed by GPT-6 Astra under the authors' guidance.

  • Abstract: In this paper, we introduce low probability of target detection (LPTD) as a new security objective for wireless sensing systems, where the existence of a target is concealed from passive wardens. Unlike conventional communication security and covert communication, LPTD aims to protect the sensed target itself from malicious detection, rather than concealing transmitted signals or communication activities. Specifically, we propose a reconfigurable-antenna-based reflector-assisted target covertness scheme, where passive reflectors deployed around the target are harnessed as artificial scattering sources to confuse the warden. By adjusting their reflection coefficients, these reflectors reshape the local scattering environment and mask the target-induced echo. We then analyze the warden's optimal detection strategy and derive the false-alarm and missed-detection probabilities, followed by a closed-form expression for its minimum total detection error probability. Numerical results validate the analysis and show that sufficient cooperation among reflectors can increase the warden's minimum total detection error probability to approximately 0.94--0.96, making target detection close to random guessing.

We demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed scheme through a case study, proving that absolute target covertness can be achieved without consuming dedicated jamming power. Finally, we outline key open challenges and promising research directions to realize truly secure, ubiquitous sensing in future ISAC ecosystems.

Title: CoordX Types: A Trustless, Swappable Type System for Shared Records

  • Authors: David Hyland-Wood, Rob Ewin
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16128
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.16128
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.16128]] CoordX Types: A Trustless, Swappable Type System for Shared Records(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16128)
  • Keywords: security
  • Abstract: The CoordX Protocol carries signed and encrypted entries between parties. It proves who wrote each entry and who had permission to write it. It does not say what an entry means. That task belongs above the protocol layer. This paper describes the CoordX type system, the layer that gives entries a meaning. A type is a short document that says how to read one part of a shared record: which entries count, what each position means, what a party may do there, and what a party must do next. The type system has two key properties. It is trustless: a type is an ordinary signed object fixed by its digest, and every party evaluates it for itself over entries that the CoordX Protocol has already verified. No party has to trust a server, a registry or another party's software. It is swappable: a type names the interpreter it is written for, so many interpreters, of different capabilities and designs, can be used side by side in one record. We describe how to write type schemas, and we define the expressive power of the schema language. Payloads form a regular language. Entry structure forms a regular tree language. Every rule and every computed value is a total function in the primitive recursive class, so evaluation always halts. We compare this with KQML, FIPA-ACL and CBCL, and we relate it to language-theoretic security.

  • Abstract: Hardware fault injection attacks threaten machine learning systems, yet their impact on reinforcement learning (RL) has received little attention despite the use of RL in safety-critical applications. Moving beyond benchmark environments, this work evaluates an online traffic light controller in a microscopic traffic simulator, where a single injected infinite value degrades accumulated vehicle waiting time by 148x while corrupting under 4% of the Q-table, and where triple modular redundancy corrected the faults injected in our tests, although glitching could still halt the device. Title: PolicySniff: Inferring Agent Policy States through Shared-GPU Execution

  • Authors: Weimin Fu, Minghao Shao, Zeng Wang, Qiying Li, Yuntao Liu, Johann Knechtel, Ozgur Sinanoglu, Muhammad Shafique, Ramesh Karri, Xiaolong Guo

  • Abstract: LLM agents apply approval settings, such as whether to ask before a consequential tool call, through instructions in their system prompt, and operators keep these settings confidential by restricting access to prompts and responses. PolicySniff infers an agent's approval-policy state through shared-GPU execution. The policy prefix is cached before capture, so the attacker observes processing of a task suffix that is identical under both settings. On an eager sparse mixture-of-experts runtime, where each selected expert runs as separate work, a frozen and blinded study identified the deployed setting at 94.17% balanced accuracy on its primary estimand. Across all authorization conditions, accuracy was 88.82% on tasks for which both settings produce the same decision and 91.98% on tasks where they differ. Hardware replays of expert routing show that its temporal density and layer-and-bucket alignment carry the policy information. Behavioral compliance does not imply configuration confidentiality: deployments that keep their approval setting private should measure its distinguishability on the serving runtime and enforce authorization outside the model.

Title: When can an interpretability claim be trusted? Stability and certification of feature dimensionality, ridge leverage and the feature recovery rate

  • Authors: Ali Tajfar, Behnam Tajfar
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16190
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.16190
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.16190]] When can an interpretability claim be trusted? Stability and certification of feature dimensionality, ridge leverage and the feature recovery rate(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16190)
  • Keywords: interpretability
  • Abstract: Interpretability tools increasingly support safety claims, such as that a sparse dictionary has recovered most of a model's features. Such claims rest on measurements with arbitrary thresholds, and small changes to the model weights, a different training seed or a different threshold can reverse them. We study two widely used measurements in toy models and synthetic dictionaries with known ground truth: the feature dimensionality of Elhage et al. and the feature recovery rate (FRR) with a cosine threshold. We characterize exactly where feature dimensionality is discontinuous and replace it with a regularized (ridge) version with sharp stability bounds: the ridge leverage score changes by at most $3\sqrt{3}/(8\sqrt{\lambda})$ times the operator-norm change of the weights, and this constant cannot be improved; for the regularized dimensionality the stability constant grows like $\rho/4$ in the scale $\rho=R/\sqrt{\lambda}$, with explicit upper and lower bounds. We also prove certificates stating when a claim "feature $i$ is recovered" must survive a given change of the dictionary. The certificates held in every test and are informative for small perturbations, but certify no feature non-trivially across independently trained dictionaries; at the standard threshold, whether a feature counts as recovered changed with the training seed for 55 percent of features. Experiments use toy models only. The proofs, code and text were drafted by AI agents directed by the authors and checked by an independent blind AI reviewer and by numerical tests; the authors take full responsibility for the content.

Title: WorldDraft: If Code Builds the World, Can Video Models Just Render It?

  • Authors: Zhiyu Hou, Wen-Jie Shu, Harold Haodong Chen, Weilin Ruan, Chenfei Liao, Liuzhou Zhang, Disen Lan, Xiaogang Xu, Qi Chen, Ser-Nam Lim

  • Copy Paste: [[2611.16217]] WorldDraft: If Code Builds the World, Can Video Models Just Render It?(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16217)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Multimodal coding agents decouple 4D world construction from visual generation, repurposing video models from world generators toward generative renderers. This division of labor raises a fundamental question: if code builds the world, can video models just render it? A reliable generative renderer hinges on two complementary criteria: (i) world preservation, retaining prescribed 4D kinematics and camera paths; and (ii) visual realization, persistently binding text-specified target attributes over time. To evaluate this capability, we introduce WorldDraft, a benchmark of $432$ strictly paired test cases across $48$ executable 4D world families spanning six dynamic challenge categories. To mitigate these bottlenecks, we propose WorldDraft-Primer, a plug-and-play visual prompting strategy that consistently improves both objectives across frozen video renderers. Together, WorldDraft and WorldDraft-Primer lay a foundation for bridging programmatic 4D simulation with video generation, advancing physically grounded generative rendering.

Title: Disentangled Embedding Optimization for Diffusion-Based Dataset Distillation

  • Authors: Hongcheng Li, Yucan Zhou, Yibin Wang, Xiaoyan Gu, Bo Li, Weiping Wang
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16224
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.16224
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.16224]] Disentangled Embedding Optimization for Diffusion-Based Dataset Distillation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16224)
  • Keywords: diffusion, generative
  • Abstract: Dataset distillation aims to compress large-scale datasets into compact synthetic subsets that enable efficient model training with comparable performance to the original data. Recent advances show that diffusion models are highly effective for this task due to their strong generative capability. Existing diffusion-based approaches typically perform class-wise clustering and use the obtained cluster prototypes as diffusion inputs to generate distilled samples. However, the uniformly distributed original samples in a category inevitably lead to entanglement near cluster boundaries, pulling the cluster prototypes closer and reducing the inter-cluster separation. Consequently, the diffusion model receives overly similar latent inputs, resulting in synthetic data with limited diversity. To address this issue, we propose Disentangled Embedding Optimization (DEO), a simple yet effective framework that replaces static clustering with trainable embeddings. These embeddings are optimized to match the Gaussian statistics (i.e., mean and covariance) of the original data distribution, while a diversity regularizer is designed to encourage separation among these embeddings to improve the intra-class variability. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our proposed DEO can generate more diversified and representative distilled data than existing cluster-based diffusion methods, achieving state-of-the-art performance and superior generalization across multiple datasets.

Title: SEC: One-Share Everywhere-Short Secret Sharing from Lossless Expanders

  • Authors: Muhammad Arzaki, Muhammed F. Esgin, Amin Sakzad, Ron Steinfeld
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.IT
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16244
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.16244
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.16244]] SEC: One-Share Everywhere-Short Secret Sharing from Lossless Expanders(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16244)
  • Keywords: security, privacy, protect
  • Abstract: We introduce SEC, an expander-code-based ramp secret sharing scheme over $\mathbb{Z}$-modules, particularly suitable for lattice-based distributed cryptography. It is everywhere-short: bounded secret coefficients yield bounded share coefficients, and the integer reconstruction vectors have bounded entries. Moreover, each participant receives exactly one share, regardless of the number of parties and the privacy and reconstruction thresholds. This improves on prior schemes with bounded share coefficients and bounded entries in the integer reconstruction vectors, in which the number of sub-shares or shares per participant grows either with the number of parties and the threshold or with the security parameter. The construction rests on a coding-theoretic observation: in the bi-adjacency matrix of a lossless bipartite expander, unique-neighbor peeling completes every sufficiently small set of columns to a square unimodular submatrix. We prove correctness, everywhere-shortness, and linearity, and give a publicly computable entrywise bound on the reconstruction vector our algorithm returns for each authorized set. For privacy, we introduce hard-core local leakage resilience, which strengthens the leakage-resilience notion of Nielsen and Simkin (EUROCRYPT 2020) to protect a hard-core function of the secret, and we prove that SEC satisfies it against correlated multi-query local leakage under the MatrixHint-MLWE problem, which admits a reduction from the standard MLWE problem. For $n=60$ participants and security parameter $\kappa=128$, SEC achieves privacy threshold 6 against $2^{64}$ leakage queries and empirical reconstruction threshold 46. The ideal entropy-coded secret and share sizes are about 3.5 KiB and 6.4 KiB, respectively.

  • Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) can generate fluent claims that are unsupported by a given source document, motivating accurate and efficient document-grounded factuality detection. Title: Rectified Looped Transformer

  • Authors: Musheng Li, Jiawei Zhang, Ziyuan Liu, Yuantao Gu
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16265
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.16265
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.16265]] Rectified Looped Transformer(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16265)
  • Keywords: robust, transformer
  • Abstract: Looped Transformers increase effective depth by repeatedly applying a shared block, offering a parameter-efficient approach to language modeling. However, most existing works directly reuse each loop's output as the next's input, which can lead to hidden-state scale growth and perturbation amplification as recurrence deepens. We propose Rectified Looped Transformer (RLT), which redesigns feedback between loops using an interpolation schedule inspired by flow-based modeling. At each loop, RLT forms the next input by linearly interpolating between the current state and its prediction. Such interpolation distributes state changes across loops, encouraging a smoother trajectory from the initial input to the final output. Theoretical analysis proves that, under appropriate assumptions, RLT keeps state magnitudes uniformly bounded across loops and reduces the worst-case perturbation amplification. We evaluate RLT on language modeling corpora and downstream benchmarks. At matched training and inference depths, RLT consistently achieves lower perplexity than a standard looped Transformer, while exhibiting greater robustness to input perturbations and variance in inference depth. Consistent with the theoretical results, latent trajectory analysis shows that RLT maintains more stable hidden-state norms across loops, which also facilitates extrapolation-based decoding strategies. Notably, RLT achieves these gains without requiring additional trainable parameters or extra recurrent block evaluations, thus providing a simple yet more stable feedback mechanism for looped Transformers.

However, each denoising step requires a full forward pass over long multimodal sequences in which visual tokens dominate the sequence length, and a fixed step budget further compounds this quadratic per-step cost. Existing inference acceleration approaches for multimodal dLLMs characterize attention redundancy implicitly, motivating designs from isolated attention visualizations rather than from measurement. We close this gap with DRAMA, a training-free acceleration framework grounded in a systematic dissection of attention redundancy in multimodal dLLMs. By probing attention tensors along spatial and temporal axes, we reveal two persistent redundancy structures: (i) attention mass consistently concentrates on a sparse subset of visual tokens, and (ii) attention distributions rapidly stabilize across denoising steps after an initial formation phase. These findings motivate two complementary mechanisms: Spatial-Redundancy-Aware Visual Token Pruning (SVTP) and Sufficiency-Guided Adaptive Stopping (SGAS). Together, DRAMA achieves an average 18.67 times inference speedup across six vision-language benchmarks without training, architectural modification, or attention caching, while maintaining competitive accuracy. Our results establish a strong, measurement-grounded acceleration baseline for multimodal dLLM inference.

Title: RADIANT: Redundancy-Aware Information Allocation for Universal Multi-Modal Fusion

  • Authors: Shenghai Wu, Yue Lin
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.MM
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16382
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.16382

  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Universal multi-modal fusion often fails not because modalities are insufficiently aligned, but because the fusion process allocates information poorly: redundant evidence is repeatedly amplified, modality-unique evidence is over-smoothed during alignment, and task-dependent synergistic evidence is insufficiently activated. We present RADIANT (Redundancy-Aware Dynamic Information Allocation Network), a task-general framework that organizes fusion around three complementary roles: suppressing redundant cross-modal overlap, preserving modality-unique evidence, and composing task-conditioned synergy through cross-modal interaction. RADIANT also exhibits strong direct cross-dataset transfer, with further gains from inference-time task-memory adaptation while keeping all network weights frozen. Additional ablations and sensitivity studies support the robustness of the allocation mechanism.

  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16409

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: It is common to train Large Language Models (LLMs) for agentic tasks with Reinforcement Learning (RL) from scalar rewards. Across agentic benchmarks, \acerl{} achieves higher held-out performance than baseline methods. The results suggest that agent training should optimize not only the actions in the current rollout, but also the model-written experience that future rollouts inherit.

Title: SoScale: Second-Order Scale Search for FP4 Quantization

  • Authors: Pengxiang Zhao, Xing Li, Hui-Ling Zhen, Weizhe Lin, Xianzhi Yu, Zhenhua Dong
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16426
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.16426
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.16426]] SoScale: Second-Order Scale Search for FP4 Quantization(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16426)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Low-bit quantization reduces the memory footprint and inference cost of large language models (LLMs), yet accurate FP4 quantization remains challenging. Practical formats such as MXFP4, NVFP4, and HiF4 pair a 4-bit floating-point payload with block-wise or hierarchical scale codes, making accuracy highly sensitive to how each local distribution is scaled. Default maximum-absolute-value scaling preserves dynamic range, but can spend scarce FP4 levels on rarely occupied outlier regions and leave dense tensor values poorly resolved. Existing scale-search methods minimize local element-wise reconstruction error, optimizing tensor fidelity rather than layer-output fidelity. We propose SoScale, a second-order weight scale-code search method for hierarchical FP4 quantization. SoScale searches hardware-valid scale codes under a Hessian-aware layer reconstruction objective and couples this search with GPTQ-style error recovery, selecting scales by their impact on layer outputs. Across Qwen3 models, SoScale consistently reduces downstream quantization degradation under MXFP4, NVFP4, and HiF4.

  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Time series forecasting aims to predict the future evolution of complex systems by learning their underlying dynamics from past observations. Despite recent advances in deep learning, Transformers in particular still struggle to capture the temporal evolution of system dynamics. Existing approaches have been largely driven by architectural innovation, with comparatively little attention paid to training strategies that explicitly exploit the temporal structure governing system dynamics. In this work, we introduce DeltaTSF, a dynamics-aware forecasting framework based on discrete derivative representations of time series. Specifically, DeltaTSF jointly learns the series and their discrete temporal derivatives across both input and forecast horizons within a unified, model-agnostic framework. This formulation enables the model to better reflect the underlying system dynamics, resulting in more stable and physically coherent forecasts. Notably, DeltaTSF does not require explicit derivative computation and introduces no additional computational overhead at inference time. Extensive experiments across diverse benchmarks and backbone architectures demonstrate that DeltaTSF consistently improves forecasting performance and yields more dynamically coherent predictions than strong SOTA methods. These results highlight the effectiveness of enforcing dynamic coherence through derivative representations as a model-agnostic paradigm that operates alongside and complements architectural design.   • Keywords: security, defense, attack
  • Abstract: We consider a physical layer authentication scenario in which a legitimate user (Alice) communicates with an authen- ticator (Bob) through a reconfigurable intelligent surface (RIS), controlled by Bob. An attacker (Trudy) aims to impersonate Alice by suitably precoding her messages and still transmitting them through the RIS. This two-part study analyzes this attack-defense interaction between Bob and Trudy under perfect (Part I) and partial (Part II) channel knowledge by all the agents. Part I formulates the joint RIS selection and precoder design under perfect channel knowledge as a finite zero-sum game, establishing exact channel subspace conditions for attack indistinguishability alongside equilibrium mixed strategies. Part II extends the defense to incomplete channel information, where each agent observes only its own channel, by formulating a finite-action Bayesian game and characterizing the resulting Bayesian Nash equilibrium. Simulations in geometric and 3GPP TR 38.901 UMi-NLOS channels reveal that authentication performance is governed by available spatial degrees of freedom, channel correlation, and operating signal-to-noise ratio. The proposed equilibrium strategies effectively balance communication rate and security, outperforming conventional benchmark policies.

  • Abstract: Cameras and LiDARs (Light Detection and Ranging) provide complementary observations of the same scene: cameras capture rich appearance cues while LiDAR sensors provide accurate geometric measurements. On robotic platforms, residual reprojection errors of several pixels are commonly observed even after calibration due to temporal desynchronization, mechanical vibrations, or inaccuracies in point cloud reconstruction. ReFineR builds upon differentiable point splatting rasterization and a smooth Gradient Orientation Measure (GOM) to iteratively refine the camera pose through analysis-by-synthesis optimization. We evaluated the method on a curated dataset of manually calibrated images collected from heterogeneous data sources. Furthermore, we show that an automatic point cloud rescale factor can be estimated prior to optimization, enabling robust refinement across a wide range of scenarios using a single scale-invariant formulation.   • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.16588

  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: Affected-version ranges in vulnerability databases decide which deployments scanners, patching pipelines, and incident responders treat as vulnerable, yet these ranges are often incomplete, so vulnerable releases are silently treated as safe. This cross-version PoC migration combines source-code analysis, environment repair, and execution-based verification. In one configuration, withholding the reference PoC and its environment specification reduces resolve rate from 50.4% to 15.0%, highlighting the value of the supplied reference package. CrossPloit provides an execution-based measurement of under-reported exposure and of how far its enumeration can be automated.

Title: When Chekhov's Gun Fires Anyway: Over-Realization in Text-to-Image Models

  • Authors: Jiahao Huang, Kon Woo Kim, Song-Duo Ma, Yuka Kitamura, Felix Wagner, Yunshan Huang, Sunisth Kumar, Xanh Ho, Yun-Ang Wu, Zhijie Chen, Yunjia Xu, Junfeng Jiang, Zefan Yu, Fei Cheng, Akiko Aizawa

  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: In text-to-image generation, a target that the prompt mentions often appears in the generated image in a way the prompt does not call for. We call this failure over-realization. Mental-spaces theory points to the situations where it can happen: under a negation, inside what a character believes or imagines, as the vehicle of a figurative comparison, or behind a viewpoint that hides the target. Prior work has studied only negation in depth, and the conventional alignment and preference metrics do not penalize the failure. We build OverReal to measure it: OverReal-Gen, 698 prompts covering the four situations, on which we measure each generator's over-realization rate; and OverReal-Det, 2,841 generated images human-labeled for whether and how the target is over-realized, on which we verify the automatic judge that produces this rate. Our evaluation of eight text-to-image systems reveals that all of them over-realize, and that conventional metrics such as CLIPScore reward the failure rather than penalize it. Prompt expansion only partly mitigates the failure. Inside the generator, the diffusion backbone attends to a mentioned target almost as much as to a requested one. The cue words that should hold the target back receive less attention than ordinary words.

Title: Manifold Attracting Flow Matching: Physics-Constrained Sampling via Continuous Geometric Dynamics

  • Subjects: cs.LG

  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.16677

  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Generating scientific data under previously unseen equality constraints requires satisfying the prescribed constraints without destroying the physical structure learned from data. Existing inference-time approaches often enforce physical constraints by repeatedly correcting predicted endpoints, but such discrete corrections can distort learned generative structure and introduce unstable feedback. In this work, we propose Manifold Attracting Flow Matching (MAFM), which continuously evolves a persistent anchor through an ODE whose dynamics drive it analytically toward the constraint manifold. Specifically, its geometric correction preserves model-guided tangent motion while prescribing the normal motion to contract constraint violations toward the manifold. This construction separates model tracking from constraint attraction and provides theoretical guarantees for residual contraction and local feedback stabilization. MAFM provides a flexible framework for coordinating learned physical structure and explicit constraints through controllable sampling dynamics, without retraining the underlying generative model.

Title: MobileProbe: Adaptive Security Evaluation of Mobile Agents via Trajectory-Aware Injection

  • Authors: Yingbin Jin, Ji'an Wang, HaoYang Chai, Hanjun Luo, Li Hu, Xinfeng Li, Huadi Zheng, PeiZhao Hu, Qingqing Ye, Haibo Hu

  • Keywords: security, attack
  • Abstract: Mobile GUI agents read the screen of a phone and operate apps on a user's behalf to complete multi-step tasks such as online shopping or messaging. Because the agent acts on whatever the screen shows, an attacker can embed malicious instructions into ordinary UI content and redirect its actions. We refer to this threat as environmental injection attack (EIA). Existing benchmarks measure this risk by pinning hand-crafted payloads to a fixed step of a fixed task. This approach faces two limitations. First, each payload is bound to one task in one app and does not transfer without manual redesign. Second, agents given the same task may follow different routes, so an agent that never reaches the injected page is recorded as safe without being tested. In this work, we propose MobileProbe, a trajectory-aware red-teaming framework that evaluates mobile agents adaptively. MobileProbe discovers attacker-controllable content surfaces through automated pentesting, localizes vulnerable decision points along the agent's trajectory, and iteratively refines context-aware payloads through a surrogate-filtered evolutionary loop. We construct a benchmark of 83 long-horizon attack instances spanning 12 mobile applications. Each is annotated with a Confidentiality, Integrity, or Availability (CIA) attack goal to cover the principal categories of security threats facing mobile agents. On three representative agent architectures, MobileProbe attains higher attack success rates than human-written, zero-shot LLM, and prior automated red-teaming baselines adapted to the mobile domain. MobileProbe can serve as a pre-deployment auditing tool to systematically uncover vulnerabilities and inform security hardening of mobile agents.

  • Authors: Kazuma Ikeda, Kotaro Oishi, Ryosei Hara, Ryo Yoshida, Takahiro Kado, Ibuki Fujioka, Taro Beppu, Mariko Isogawa, Kentaro Yoshioka

  • Abstract: LiDAR is a core sensor for autonomous driving, yet it is highly vulnerable to fog. Full-waveform LiDAR (FWL), which records the received optical signal before peak detection, retains these weak returns, but the lack of suitable data has prevented research on recovering fog-hidden objects from FWL. We present Fog-FWL, a dataset foundation for this task that consists of a real-world benchmark, Fog-FWL-Real, and a large-scale synthetic training set, Fog-FWL-Sim. Fog-FWL-Sim contains 12,000 frames generated by our physics-based simulator, Fog-FWL-Simulator, which integrates spatially heterogeneous fog density fields, time-resolved light transport including multiple scattering, and a photon-counting SPAD receiver model. The two subsets share the same sensor format and annotation protocol, enabling a strict sim-to-real evaluation in which no real waveforms are used for training. Our analysis shows that, under the hardest condition, object returns are included in the three strongest peaks for only 34.9% of object rays, underscoring the need for waveform-level recovery. Fog-FWL-Real, Fog-FWL-Sim, and the Fog-FWL-Simulator will be publicly released to facilitate research on fog-robust LiDAR perception.

Title: Class-Competence Aggregation: Output-Layer Routing for Federated Multiclass DDoS Detection under Class-Concentrated Non-IID Data

  • Authors: Tapu Datta, Hafiz Imtiaz
  • Subjects: cs.LG, stat.ML
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16772
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.16772
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.16772]] Class-Competence Aggregation: Output-Layer Routing for Federated Multiclass DDoS Detection under Class-Concentrated Non-IID Data(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16772)
  • Keywords: attack, federate
  • Abstract: Federated learning over distributed intrusion-detection sensors naturally produces \emph{class-concentrated} non-IID data, where an entire attack class is held by a single client and absent elsewhere. We show this collapses federated averaging in a specific, localized way: the output layer holds one decision boundary per class, and when a class's aggregation weight comes overwhelmingly from clients that have never seen it, its neuron receives an active anti-signal rather than mere dilution, collapsing to zero. On CIC-DDoS2019, standard FedAvg drives the concentrated LDAP class to $0.00 \pm 0.00\%$ F1 across every seed. We propose Class-Competence Aggregation (CCA), which aggregates the output layer per class using each client's own locally reported validation F1 as the weight, while hidden layers remain under standard size-weighted FedAvg. CCA requires no server-held auxiliary data and adds only one F1 scalar per class per round beyond the feature-scaling and class-count exchanges common to any federated pipeline. On our primary CNN-LSTM architecture, CCA alone raises LDAP F1 from $0\%$ to $84.83 \pm 1.65\%$, and the full method reaches $91.64 \pm 0.57\%$ macro F1, closing the federated-to-centralized gap from $-42.53$ to $-4.05$ points. Benchmarked against four other federated methods under identical partitions and seeds, CCA is the only one that recovers the concentrated class substantially and reliably; a $178\times$ smaller MLP shows the same qualitative recovery, though less completely, confirming the effect holds across architecture scale even as its magnitude varies. Results are validated across three seeds and replicated on a second dataset, CICIDS2017.

Title: Signed Dyadic Path Encoding for Efficient Non-Interactive Private Decision Tree Evaluation over TFHE

  • Authors: Xintong Song, Yiqiang Zhao, Qizhi Zhang, Yao Li, Jiaji He, Fei Zhao
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16818
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.16818
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.16818]] Signed Dyadic Path Encoding for Efficient Non-Interactive Private Decision Tree Evaluation over TFHE(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16818)
  • Keywords: security
  • Abstract: Private decision-tree evaluation lets a server classify a client's encrypted query without learning the input. In the non-interactive, path-based setting over TFHE, the server returns a single encrypted prediction by aggregating per-path comparison outcomes, and the standard per-leaf formulation pays one programmable bootstrap (PBS) per leaf, which dominates the cost. We propose a signed dyadic path-sum encoding that nearly halves this cost: each internal node receives a signed dyadic label so that every root-to-leaf path accumulates a distinct integer sum, which lets the two children of a leaf-parent be packed into a single test polynomial and resolved by one PBS, reducing the PBS count from the number of leaves to the number of leaf-parents. We formalize the distinct-sum property and prove that the dyadic labeling is the range-minimal encoding satisfying it, and its half-symmetric signed form is magnitude-optimal, tightening the comparison-stage noise bound at fixed parameters. We derive the two conditions under which the encrypted evaluation returns the exact prediction and instantiate parameters with per-inference failure below $2^{-64}$ at 128-bit security. We show that the ring degree grows linearly with the consumed plaintext range, which confines the profitable regime to depths $\leq 4$, where our method stays faster even when each scheme runs at its own cost-optimal parameters. We further extend the encoding to gradient-boosted ensembles. On standard UCI benchmarks at depths 1-4, our method reduces single-tree latency by $1.51\times$-$1.92\times$ and ensemble latency by $2\times$, without accuracy loss, while preserving the compact, non-interactive response.

Title: Shortcuts or Memorisation? Revisiting Data Corruption in Natural Language Understanding with Large Language Models

  • Authors: Aarne Talman, Stergios Chatzikyriakidis
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16843
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.16843
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.16843]] Shortcuts or Memorisation? Revisiting Data Corruption in Natural Language Understanding with Large Language Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16843)
  • Keywords: robust, large language model
  • Abstract: Pre-trained encoders fine-tuned on natural language inference (NLI) and other language understanding tasks keep much of their accuracy after the input is perturbed, e.g. by removing all nouns or shuffling the words. Does the same hold for large language models (LLMs), and if so, is it because they have seen the benchmarks during pre-training? In this paper we revisit these perturbation experiments with eight LLMs alongside fine-tuned encoders on GLUE and the three rounds of ANLI. Regardless of model size, every system that answers directly retains 36-72% of its above-chance performance without nouns on MNLI compared to only 14-29% on ANLI. Only a model that reasons before answering departs from this pattern. We also check contamination indicators (including exact $n$-gram counts in the public pre-training data of two of the models) and find no link between exposure and robustness. Our strongest evidence comes from 368 new, human-validated items that none of the models can have seen. All eight LLMs keep more of their performance without nouns on items written in MNLI's style than on items written without its cues. When the perturbation instead removes the function words carrying such cues, this advantage reverses. In light of these results, we argue that robustness to perturbation on familiar NLI benchmarks reflects dataset shortcuts rather than memorisation, and tells us more about the benchmark than about the model.
