Title: Look It Up: Multi-bit LLM Watermarking via Codebook Search

  • Authors: Jarosław Janas, Paweł Morawiecki, Josef Pieprzyk
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16890
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.16890
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.16890]] Look It Up: Multi-bit LLM Watermarking via Codebook Search(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16890)
  • Keywords: robust, extraction, watermark
  • Abstract: Attributing short LLM-generated texts to a specific source remains challenging for existing multi-bit watermarking methods, which often require long generations or costly decoding to recover payloads reliably. We propose a codebook-based watermarking scheme that maps an $n$-bit payload to a longer $u$-bit codeword and embeds it during generation through token-level logit biasing. During extraction, the received bit sequence is matched to the nearest codeword in a precomputed secret codebook using Hamming or Levenshtein distance. Experiments show that our method achieves near-perfect recovery for 16-24 bit payloads on 200-token generations and strong performance on 127-token generations for base models. Results are weaker on some instruction-tuned models with very short texts, where low-entropy output distributions reduce embedding reliability. We also study a BCH-based variant that avoids codebook storage but generally reduces robustness. The Levenshtein variant improves resilience to deletions and light paraphrasing, while insertions and stronger rewrites remain challenging. We analyze collision probability, codeword separation, extraction time, tokenization consistency, and text quality, highlighting both the practicality and limitations of codebook-based watermarking for short-form attribution.

Title: Factored One-sided Adam-Moment for Practical and Scalable SOAP

  • Authors: Jaemyung Yu, Byeongho Heo, Sangdoo Yun, Dongyoon Han
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16905
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.16905
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.16905]] Factored One-sided Adam-Moment for Practical and Scalable SOAP(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16905)
  • Keywords: robust, transformer, large language model
  • Abstract: Second-order optimizers (e.g., Shampoo, SOAP) have emerged as compelling alternatives to Adam/AdamW and promise faster per-step convergence than AdamW in transformer training. However, they still remain costly in practice, and even SOAP still pays substantial optimizer-state memory and eigendecomposition overhead. We ask whether the benefits of SOAP can be retained at nearly the efficiency of AdamW. In this paper, we answer yes with a lightweight SOAP variant, Factored One-sided Adam-Moment (FOAM). The path to practical impact is twofold: first, FOAM employs input-only preconditioning, dropping the output-side Kronecker factor to reduce memory and remove one eigendecomposition. The Kronecker structure of the Hessian shows that this is exact for a diagonal output-side curvature and nearly lossless for a near-diagonal one, as we observe empirically. Second, FOAM replaces SOAP's dense rotated second moment with a factored Adam-moment estimator based on row-by-column statistics. Under the K-FAC Fisher assumption with a diagonal output curvature, this estimator exactly recovers SOAP's dense second moment, and we measure only a 15-20% K-FAC violation, with negligible effect on the update. Our empirical results support the claim that FOAM matches strong SOAP and Muon baselines in validation loss across scales of large language model (LLM) pre-training, uses half of SOAP's optimizer state, runs at Muon-class wall-clock time, and retains robustness across a three-fold learning-rate range. The results hold from 124M to 760M parameters, demonstrating that SOAP's gains need not require SOAP's cost. Code: this https URL

Title: STEALTH: Steganographic Text Encoding Agnostic to Low-Level Tensor Hardware

  • Authors: William Fleshman
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16928
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.16928
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.16928]] STEALTH: Steganographic Text Encoding Agnostic to Low-Level Tensor Hardware(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16928)
  • Keywords: secure, robust, steal
  • Abstract: Provably secure linguistic steganography hides a message inside text sampled from a language model, so that without the key, a stegotext is computationally indistinguishable from an honest model sample. Nearly every such scheme assumes the receiver can recompute the sender's token probabilities exactly bit for bit. We show this assumption is fragile: across three model families, bit-exact schemes decode almost none of our messages once the receiver's floating-point precision, quantization, or GPU architecture differs from the sender's, or even when the receiver merely scores a text in parallel versus one token at a time, with the same model, precision, and hardware held fixed. We present STEALTH, a scheme built for exactly this setting. The sender draws each token with an exponential race driven by keyed pseudorandom noise, so a receiver's probability mismatch flips a token only when the race is close; a rateless spinal code, decoded by beam search, absorbs the resulting flips without the sender and receiver ever agreeing on a single distribution. We prove stegotexts remain indistinguishable from honest samples under this construction. Empirically, STEALTH decodes every message we sent for every receiver in the sender's model family, at every precision and quantization level, on smaller models, and on a different GPU architecture. STEALTH is even robust across different model families with separate vocabularies and tokenizers, decoding every message we tested. Our approach carries far more payload per token than prior robust schemes, and trained steganalyzers and a targeted statistical test both fail to distinguish its stegotexts from honest model output.

  • Keywords: robust, large language model
  • Abstract: The easy access to Large Language Models (LLMs) has made AI-generated texts on the web increasingly ubiquitous, making AI-text detection a crucial task. Prompts greatly influence the generation process, but existing detectors focus solely on the generated texts --- supervised methods learn from labeled texts only, and zero-shot methods rely on the text's probability distributions --- ignoring the underlying generation process, which limits their robustness in open-world scenarios. We introduce SPADE: Soft Prompts for Ai-text DEtection, a novel framework for detecting AI-generated text that transfers better across datasets. Specifically, we measure the prompt's influence and use it as a discriminative signal. We characterize the prompt's influence by introducing a Pointwise Mutual Information (PMI) term that captures how much a prompt explains a text. Based on this quantity, SPADE trains one soft prompt per class to amplify the separation, and predicts the class whose prompt best explains the candidate text. Extensive experiments on several benchmarks against various detectors demonstrate the robustness and effectiveness of our method, achieving state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance on out-of-distribution (OOD) datasets.

Title: Anonymous in Name Only: A Critical Review and a Reidentification Exercise on the European Commission's Anonymization Measures for Google Search Data under Article 6(11) of the DMA

  • Keywords: privacy, protect, attack
  • Abstract: In July 2026, the European Commission (EC) set out the anonymization measures Alphabet must implement to share Google Search data with competing search engines under Article 6(11) of the Digital Markets Act. The key advance is Model-Initiated Conversation (MIC), which enables the model to identify topics worth discussing in the evolving visual stream and initiate conversations at appropriate moments without a user query. Stream-Aware Tool Calling (SATC) enables the model to invoke external tools using the current visual and conversational context and incorporate returned information into the ongoing interaction. Interactive Visual Grounding (IVG) supports visual grounding in both user inputs and model outputs. The model can identify objects marked by users on video frames and produce frame-aligned bounding boxes to link its responses to specific objects and regions. To develop these capabilities, we construct a dedicated streaming interaction dataset and adopt a three-stage training scheme to integrate the new behaviors with AURA's existing streaming interaction abilities. We further incorporate Long-Horizon Interaction Memory (LHIM) to maintain contextual continuity over extended video streams. Evaluations on public streaming benchmarks show that AURA 2.0 achieves leading performance among open-source streaming models, while targeted evaluations further validate its new interaction capabilities. Title: NonSense: On the (In)security of FPGA Anti-tamper Sensors

  • Authors: Mehmet Ali Cetin, Fatemeh Khojasteh Dana, Saleh Khalaj Monfared, Nima Seyed Aghazadeh, Maryam Saadat Safa, Shahin Tajik
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17065
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.17065
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.17065]] NonSense: On the (In)security of FPGA Anti-tamper Sensors(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17065)
  • Keywords: security, attack
  • Abstract: FPGAs are widely used in mission-critical systems, where a successful physical attack can have serious consequences. This makes resilience to such attacks a critical security requirement. To defend against active physical attacks, such as glitching attacks, modern FPGAs rely on on-chip sensors that detect these disturbances and trigger responses, such as key zeroization, before a device is compromised. These sensors are deployed as both hard-IP blocks and soft-IP cores, and their trustworthiness is often taken for granted. In this work, we challenge that assumption and argue that the sensors themselves are a fragile and underexplored attack surface. Drawing on recent results, we show that both the detection and response mechanisms of state-of-the-art sensors can be bypassed without invasive tampering. We further argue that sensor reliability is reduced by factors rarely captured in threat models, including the spatial and frequency-dependent behavior of the system's power delivery network, which can steer a fault to a target while sparing nearby sensors; environmental conditions such as temperature, which shift detection thresholds; and long-term aging, which drifts sensor calibration and may open silent detection gaps. Finally, we propose a set of countermeasures that harden detection and response mechanisms against these effects.

Title: Optimal Single-History Tokenization Disambiguation for Provably Secure Linguistic Steganography

  • Authors: Feng Jiang
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17091
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.17091
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.17091]] Optimal Single-History Tokenization Disambiguation for Provably Secure Linguistic Steganography(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17091)
  • Keywords: secure, extraction
  • Abstract: Provably secure linguistic steganography assumes that the receiver recovers the sender's tokens, but a text channel delivers only text, and ambiguous tokenizations break extraction. Existing fixes change the distribution, synchronize whole pools of tokens at a capacity that collapses with large vocabularies, or track several token histories. We prove that if extraction is exact and error-free across full-support language models, a single-history receiver is confined to a prefix-free candidate set at every step -- even if it reads the entire future text and the sender constrains later steps -- and must therefore give up the stop entropy, the entropy of deciding whether each prefix token ends there. Fixing these stop decisions on the prefix forest with shared randomness yields coin projection: a closed-form scheme that attains the bound, works with any distribution coder in one model call per token, preserves ordinary sampling, and refines SyncPool for every key. Across English, Chinese and Japanese, ambiguous text is rare (at most 11 in 1,000 tokens), yet the stop entropy at word and morpheme prefixes takes 7.2-9.0% of the entropy at every step; SyncPool's further loss comes from space and single-byte UTF-8 hubs that merge subtrees while carrying almost no stop uncertainty. Coin projection keeps 91-93% of the string entropy for the coder at every vocabulary size; with the full vocabulary, it turns 2.16-6.85 times as much entropy into message bits as SyncPool and recovers every completed message.

Title: On cryptographically non-linear all-or-nothing transforms

  • Authors: Anantajeet Devaraj (1), Navid Nasr Esfahani (1) ((1) Toronto Metropolitan University)
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17102
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.17102
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.17102]] On cryptographically non-linear all-or-nothing transforms(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17102)
  • Keywords: secure
  • Abstract: An unconditionally secure $(t, s, v)$-all-or-nothing transform (AONT) is a bijection from $s$ input elements to $s$ output elements over an alphabet of size $v$, such that given any $s - t$ output elements, no information can be obtained about any $t$ input elements. While linear AONTs have been extensively studied, comparatively little is known about the existence of non-linear AONTs. In this paper, we investigate unconditionally secure cryptographically non-linear $(t, s, v)$-AONTs, distinguishing between non-additivity, non-affinity, and cryptographic non-linearity. To achieve this, we first present a computational method for enumerating general AONTs focusing on the case $(t, 3, 3)$-AONTs for $t = 1 and 2$, establishing the existence of non-additive AONTs. We then introduce a general construction of non-additive AONTs from linear ones using coordinate-wise symbol permutations. We measure the non-linearity of the AONT instances generated from this construction, using differential $\Delta_F$-uniformity and Walsh magnitude. Finally, we establish theoretical conditions under which $\Delta_F$-uniformity is reduced and present statistical results examining the non-linearity of AONTs obtained through our construction.

Title: Joint Null-Space Projection for Data-Free Model Merging

  • Authors: Tian Xia
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17103
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.17103
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.17103]] Joint Null-Space Projection for Data-Free Model Merging(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17103)
  • Keywords: data-free
  • Abstract: Merging independently fine-tuned checkpoints into a single multi-task model requires no data, no labels, and no gradient steps. In practice the task vectors of different tasks interfere, and the standard remedies--averaging, magnitude pruning, sign election--operate entry by entry, ignoring that each delta induces a low-dimensional update on the hidden features it acts upon. We recast merging as a subspace interference problem. For every two-dimensional weight tensor we take the top-r right-singular subspace of each task's delta--the input directions along which that task moves the representation most--and remove from each task vector the component lying in the union of the other tasks' subspaces. The resulting Joint Null-Space Projection (JNSP) is closed-form and order-independent, needs no forward pass, and is defined entirely by the checkpoint set. On eight CLIP image-classification tasks JNSP merges ViT-B/16 experts to 89.45% average accuracy without using a single image, 11.88 points above the strongest parameter-wise training-free baseline (TIES-Merging, 77.57%), 14.22 above Task Arithmetic, and 0.98 above WUDI's data-free consensus merge (88.47%). The gain holds across three backbones on the independently trained TALL release (+10.8, +12.6, +9.1 over TIES for ViT-B/16, ViT-B/32, ViT-L/14) and replicates there at 89.20%; controls confirm the source--a single shared subspace instead of per-task ones loses 2.9 points, and a sequential variant collapses to 69.6%. On RoBERTa-large the same projection underperforms plain task arithmetic, which we report rather than hide. Code is available at this https URL.

Title: Preserve the Vulnerability, Break the Alert: Testing the Robustness of CodeQL Taint Tracking with Natural Rewrites

  • Authors: Zhezhen Cao, Weiyuan Ding, Bowen Xu
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.SE
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17123
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.17123
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.17123]] Preserve the Vulnerability, Break the Alert: Testing the Robustness of CodeQL Taint Tracking with Natural Rewrites(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17123)
  • Keywords: security, robust, large language model
  • Abstract: Static application security testing (SAST) tools such as CodeQL serve as security gates in continuous integration and code review, and a gate is also read in reverse. That inference holds only if the analyzer is robust to changes that keep the vulnerability. We test this robustness for CodeQL's JavaScript taint tracking with natural, vulnerability-preserving rewrites. The boundary is not specific to CodeQL. 74% of the silencing rewrites also silence an independently built taint analyzer, and 53% escape three analyzers combined. A disappearing alert after an ordinary-looking change is therefore weak evidence that a vulnerability has been fixed, the gaps point SAST developers to narrow and concrete repair targets, and robustness to natural rewrites belongs alongside detection rate in how taint analyzers are evaluated.

Title: ISLA: A Coordinate-Free, Measure-Weighted Transformer for Boundary-Driven PDEs

  • Authors: Peter Sharpe
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CE, math-ph, physics.flu-dyn
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17141
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.17141
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.17141]] ISLA: A Coordinate-Free, Measure-Weighted Transformer for Boundary-Driven PDEs(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17141)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: A foundational principle of physics is that all coordinate systems are equally valid: the behavior of a PDE-governed system does not depend on the origin or orientation of the frame, nor on the units or mesh used to write it down, and neither should a surrogate model. Yet the neural surrogates with the lowest errors on today's aerodynamics benchmarks lack these invariances and fail under simple transformations of the same problem: for models trained on PDEs of cars in one pose (i.e., translation and rotation) and from one mesher, rotating the full PDE raises the surface-pressure error from 4% to more than 100%, and re-sampling its boundary raises it to about 30%. We introduce ISLA (Invariant Slice Attention), a slice-attention transformer for steady, boundary-driven PDEs in which no learned operation receives a coordinate and every sum over the boundary is a quadrature. Positions enter only as distances and relative angles to learned slice anchors, lengths are measured in a unit computed from the boundary, and interior points query the boundary through a tree-accelerated learned kernel without influencing one another. With every boundary cell a token and an exact boundary read, ISLA is equivariant to rigid motions and reflections, invariant to the units of its declared data, and consistent under mesh refinement, while at every setting each query is independent of all other queries. On AeroZoo, 2,816 flows that vary in shape, size, speed, and boundary conditions, its mean reference-scaled velocity and pressure errors are at least 3.1 and 3.3 times lower than any baseline's. On AirfRANS its errors are second only to those of a boundary-integral model (GLOBE) and at least 1.6 times lower than those of Transolver and GeoTransolver.

  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: We study data poisoning attacks on logistic regression with $\ell_2$-regularization, where an attacker chooses the features of a fixed number of injected observations with prescribed labels to maximize the logistic loss on the clean training data. Strong convexity of the defender's problem yields an exact reformulation through its first-order optimality conditions. Using convex conjugacy of the softplus function, we replace the sigmoid relations by a single convex optimality-gap inequality and express the attacker's objective as a concave function. A change of variables makes the stationarity condition affine, leaving bounded bilinear equalities as the only nonconvex constraints. We derive finite bounds on all variables involved in these equalities, including a global bound on the coefficient norm and tighter bounds obtained by preprocessing based on convex optimization. Experiments on eight binary classification datasets evaluate attack effectiveness and compare the two reformulations when solved with \textsf{SCIP}. Attacks obtained by local optimization degrade predictive performance on most datasets, while the convex-analytic formulation produces substantially stronger reported dual bounds within a fixed time limit.

Title: Symmetry in Time: A Representation Theory of Linear Recurrent Memory

  • Authors: Anif N. Shikder, Ramit Dey, Jan Minac, Lyle E. Muller
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.NE, math.RT
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17162
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.17162
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.17162]] Symmetry in Time: A Representation Theory of Linear Recurrent Memory(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17162)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: State space models (SSMs) are an emerging alternative to Transformers. SSMs offer linear-time inference and parallel training, but most implementations make the parallel scan efficient by making the recurrence diagonal: each channel of the hidden state is multiplied by an input-dependent real or complex number. These numbers commute, so the state records which inputs occurred but not their order. We formalize this with representation theory, which explains and fixes the state-tracking failures previously reported for SSMs. The state update defines a linear representation of the monoid of input sequences, and we show that an SSM tracks a group at every sequence length exactly when that group is a quotient of the group generated by its transition matrices. Two consequences follow. Diagonal real- and complex-valued SSMs can track only commutative groups, while the transition matrices of any SSM that can track a group must split into independent blocks over the real, complex, and quaternion numbers. We then introduce HyperSSM, which adds quaternion channels while keeping linear-time inference and parallel training. Trained only on final answers, HyperSSM solves the group tasks of recent state-tracking benchmarks exactly with at most five state numbers. This includes $S_5$ with 32 generators, where previous SSMs are at chance, and HyperSSM generalizes to sequences 25 times longer than those seen in training. Because the theory says what the transitions must encode, the learned weights of the linear recurrence can be interpreted directly. We demonstrate this by recovering the calibration of a real gyroscope, the rotations of imperfect qubit gates and the geometry of protein backbones.

Title: eDMD: Endpoint Distribution Matching for Few-Step Diffusion Distillation

  • Authors: Tong Zhao, Xianfang Zeng, Yucheng Han, Ruoyu Wang, Mingkun Lei, Gang Yu, Chi Zhang
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17194
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.17194
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.17194]] eDMD: Endpoint Distribution Matching for Few-Step Diffusion Distillation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17194)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Distribution matching distillation (DMD) has emerged as a leading paradigm for distilling diffusion models into few-step students, where an auxiliary fake score model tracks the student's distribution and the discrepancy between the teacher and fake predictions drives the student toward the teacher. In multi-step distillation, although only the student's final output is delivered, existing methods train the fake model on intermediate states or across all denoising steps. Consequently, the student's update signal becomes entangled with intermediate distributions, which can distort the matching direction and remain nonzero even after the student's final outputs already match the teacher distribution. To resolve this mismatch, we propose Endpoint Distribution Matching Distillation (eDMD), which trains a single shared fake model exclusively on the student's final outputs and uses it to correct every denoising step. Our theoretical analysis proves that this endpoint reference guarantees that the distribution matching term vanishes if and only if the student's final output distribution matches the teacher's, regardless of intermediate distributions. Beyond the fake reference, we further strengthen teacher supervision through a precise teacher (PT) that replaces single-step guided predictions with short multi-step trajectories on the final noise interval, paired with a calibrated query density for intermediate steps. Experiments on SD3-Medium, FLUX.2-klein (4B and 9B), and Qwen-Image-2.1 demonstrate that eDMD consistently outperforms existing distribution matching methods for 4-step generation.

Title: Physically Interpretable Basin Selection in Discrete-Variable Origami Design

  • Authors: Lucy Liu, Leon M. Kamp, Katia Bertoldi, L. Mahadevan
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cond-mat.mtrl-sci, cond-mat.soft
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17196
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.17196
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.17196]] Physically Interpretable Basin Selection in Discrete-Variable Origami Design(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17196)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Automatic differentiation has enabled rapid progress on continuous-variable mechanical design problems, but gradient-based optimization still struggles with discrete variables. The standard approach is to optimize using a continuous relaxation of the choices, then commit to the nearest discrete design at the end. Why this procedure selects unfavorable basins is poorly understood. Here, we introduce a practical, interpretable design problem inspired by origami, in which automatic differentiation is used to design shapes with a target functional behavior. Each origami sheet has a fixed geometry, and a bilevel optimization framework tunes which one of four discrete hinge types to place at each fold. Baseline gradient-based design settles into basins with a large discretization gap: relaxed optimization solves 97% of runs, but the final, discrete solutions succeed only half the time. We find that the gap can be relieved with a surprisingly simple modification. An initialization prior uniformly biased toward the stiff, flat hinge option boosts the final solve fraction from 49% to 69%. Applying the prior adds no additional compute and leaves the optimization process unchanged. Compared to the baseline, sheets produced with the prior have greater geometric compatibility and mechanical stiffness. Directly penalizing frustration and floppiness in the outer loss function produces an even greater improvement than the prior does, raising the solve fraction to 78% and indicating that these properties help distinguish the unfavorable basins. Our results demonstrate that loss landscapes for mechanical design can have physically meaningful basins that are not robust to discretization and cause solves to fail. Targeting these basins' physical signatures, even with just an initialization bias, helps avoid them.

Title: Objects as Addresses: Instance Binding in Vision Transformer Attention

  • Authors: Khalil Sabri, Guillaume-Alexandre Bilodeau, Nicolas Saunier, Wassim Bouachir
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17240
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.17240
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.17240]] Objects as Addresses: Instance Binding in Vision Transformer Attention(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17240)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Humans naturally parse visual scenes into coherent individual objects rather than scattered features, a capability cognitive science calls object binding. While recent work shows that pretrained Vision Transformers (ViTs) intrinsically acquire this ability, how native self-attention computations mechanistically keep object instances separate remains unresolved. By studying this across eight pretrained ViTs, we found that instance separation is expressed more strongly in attention routing than in token content: selected query-key relations distinguish individual objects more reliably than value similarity or residual representations. Inside heads, patches belonging to one individual tend to read from a common image region, which we term their attention address. Visually distinct parts of one person share an address, whereas identical duplicated instances receive separate, movable addresses. These shared destinations arise in part because query interactions with the spatial structure of keys direct attention toward consistent regions for one object and elsewhere for another. In DINOv2 and most other backbones we tested, editing this query component at the selected head redirects its attention address toward another individual. Grouping patches by this query component separates same-category instances, reaching .615 FG-ARI versus .289 for the same clustering of final-layer features, which tend to merge them.

Title: Goal Encoding in LLMs

  • Authors: Arnab Sen Sharma, Eric Todd, Sheridan Feucht, Andy Arditi, Rohit Gandikota, Natalie Shapira, Gabriele Sarti, Constanza Fierro, Lisa Schut, David Bau
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17297
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.17297
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.17297]] Goal Encoding in LLMs(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17297)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: We investigate whether and how LLMs encode goals: internal neural representations that precede and guide action, and that are flexible enough to be realized through many different execution paths. We construct a coding setting with multiple sub-tasks that allows us to evaluate goal-seeking behavior and the selection of sub-goals. In this setting, we localize an encoding of the next sub-goal in the outputs of a sparse set of attention heads. The same heads can be used to decode the LM's future actions in natural language (e.g., "I'm checking if a word is a palindrome"). We verify that this encoding is causal for next action and is plastic: transplanting it into a different environment, e.g., another programming language, triggers the same operation realized with a different sequence of tokens. The LM also sustains the encoding throughout the action's execution, which is puzzling because a transformer LM without recurrence cannot carry a state forward for more tokens than it has layers left. We propose that it does not need to. Under a non-recurrent goal-seeking (NRGS) account, the LM frequently re-reads the goal from the task specification, compares it with the progress visible in the growing context, and pursues what remains. We test the predictions of this account with targeted causal interventions.

Title: Learning When Diffusion Language Models Should Regenerate

  • Authors: Minhae Oh, Jeonghye Kim, Jinwoo Koo, Yeongoon Kim, Jimin Seo, Minsik Seol, Jungwoo Lee
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17320
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.17320
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.17320]] Learning When Diffusion Language Models Should Regenerate(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17320)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Remasking allows diffusion language models to revisit generated text, but effective correction requires knowing when and what to revise. Our diagnosis on Sudoku reveals two gaps. Hidden states contain error information that token confidence does not reliably expose, and remasking an isolated error usually reproduces it when the surrounding context remains fixed. These findings motivate DiVR , a verifier that predicts whether a partially generated sequence will reach a correct answer. Trained on the final correctness of the model's own trajectories, DiVR decides when to regenerate a block and which alternative to retain, while the generator itself stays unchanged. On five reasoning benchmarks with LLaDA-8B-Base, DiVR raises the average accuracy of the fine-tuned generator from 35.3\% to 43.4\% at $1.9$-$3.5\times$ the cost of greedy decoding, above search baselines that use $3.8$-$7.4\times$. It also complements post-training such as supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning, and generalizes to other generators, including Dream.

Title: K2V: Generative Keyframing For Physically Plausible Video Generation

  • Authors: Felix O'Mahony, Daniel Olmeda Reino, Roberto Cipolla, Ayush Tewari
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17324
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.17324
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.17324]] K2V: Generative Keyframing For Physically Plausible Video Generation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17324)
  • Keywords: diffusion, generative
  • Abstract: Video diffusion models are able to generate samples with high-quality appearance and textural qualities. However, content produced by these methods is known to exhibit significant physical errors. This is because, in a classical video generation setting, all physical reasoning must take place simultaneously and exclusively within the diffusion this http URL propose to resolve these issues by redistributing the task of physical reasoning onto a language model-backed keyframe generation module, which allows for explicit reasoning in language, and fine-grained control over the appearance of the video at important moments in the video. We then condition a video generation model with these keyframes, reducing the role of the diffusion model to the simpler task of interpolating between physically-correct intermediate states, rather than inferring an entire sequence with diffusion. Results demonstrate that our approach significantly outperforms existing approaches, achieving state-of-the-art performance on the Physics-IQ Verified benchmark and PhyGenBench.

Title: Back to Pixels: Lifting Latent Video Diffusion into Pixel Space

  • Authors: Yanke Song, Shuo Chen, Chris Rockwell, Guande He, Nan Liu, Desai Xie, Tianpei Gu, Yong-Hyun Park, Aryan V S, Dennis Yuren Menn, Ming-Hsuan Yang, Xun Huang
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17336
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.17336
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.17336]] Back to Pixels: Lifting Latent Video Diffusion into Pixel Space(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17336)
  • Keywords: diffusion, transformer
  • Abstract: Frontier video generation models work in a VAE latent space that compresses video in both space and time. This compression made training tractable, but now limits high-resolution, controllable, and interactive generation -- doubling resolution quadruples token count, conditions apply to groups of frames rather than individual ones, and each response must wait for the rest of its multi-frame block. We present PixVid, a simple yet effective method that lifts a pretrained latent video model to directly generate every frame in pixel space. PixVid keeps the diffusion transformer and replaces the VAE decoder with a convolutional head that renders its outputs into pixels. We find that predicting clean video rather than velocity, together with a larger noise shift, is key to quality. Finetuned from Wan2.1-14B, PixVid is competitive with the latent baseline within $\sim 1.3$M training clips. Working framewise in pixels, it generates at $2\times$ higher resolution with the same per-frame token count, keeps depth-conditioned video editing aligned under fast motion, and responds to each action at the next frame.
